WOMEN'S CHAT.

A "Great Little" Scotmen.—The birth of a son and heir to Lord and Lady Graham is a notable event, as in the ordinary course of events ha will become the head of two great Scots ducal houses, viz., those of Montrose and Hamilton. This fortunate little heir was indeed "born in the purple," for not only will he inherit all the honours of the Montrose dukedom, but also the vast estates of the late Duke of Hamilton, the possession of which made his :daughter, Lady Mary Hamilton one of the greatest heiresses of her day at the time of her marriage in June last to the Marquis of Graham, heir to the Duke of Montrose. If this "great little" Scotsman, who is also a great-grandson of the Duchess of Devonshire, should live to be a future Duke of Montrose, he will become the largest landowner in the British Isles. The Friends of Royalty.—Of late years, youthful Royalties have mixed far more freely than of yore, in general society,and have been permitted to make many friendships among members of the aristocracy. For instance the two daughters of the Duke and Duchess of Connaught reckon among their intimates Lady Marjorie Manners, Lady Beauchamp, Mrs. Cazalet, Miss Helen Post, and Mrs. Jack Leslie; the daughters of the late Duke of Sax-Coburg-Gotha, have intimate friends in Mrs. Wilfrid Ashley, and Miss Toupie Lowther; whilst among the Queen of Spain's greatest friends in this country may be mentioned the Ladies Marjorie, and Violet Manners, and Miss Margaret Trefusis. The Princess of Wales has from the first permitted her children to make friends among the aristocracy, and drawn for them a decidedly wide circle, so also has the Princess Royal,and this very wise decision is apparently recommending itself to Queen Maud of Norway. " Queen " of Egypt.—Lady Cromer whose return from Pharoah land has been eagerly looked forward to by her numerous relatives and friends in these Isles, has enjoyed as brilliant a reign as her predecessor, and has succeeded in making herself universally beloved. The retiring " Queen " of Egypt is a daughter of the late and sister of the present-Lord Bath, connected with all that is best in the way of English aristocracy, and in every way calculated to fill a great representative position. Lady Cromer is also a very beautiful woman, tall and stately, with gracious charming manners, and is moreover always perfectly dressed. A Colonial Novelist.—Mrs. Humphry Ward, the distinguished uovelist, who has been acting as hostess to the Colonial visitors, is heaselta daughter of Greater Britain. Her father aps a son of Dr. Arnold, of Rugby, but he emigrated to Tasmania when that country was still a penal Colony, and it was at Hobart, the capital of Tasmania, that his daughter, Mary Augusta, the Mrs. Humphry Ward of to-day was born.. The city is said to be beautifully situated, and its invigorating climate attracts many visitors from Australia. Until compara- tively recent times, it was not considered polite to inquire too closely into the family history of Colonists, owing to the fact that many of them are descended from convicts transported into Tasmania, but the oritninal taint has long since Passed away into ancient history. The Boston Waltz.—This is to be a season of dances, so say authorities on the subject, and the new waltz has certainly "caught on." This particular dance consists of three running steps, in exact three times, and if the motion is kept very smooth and gliding, it is pleasant, and certainly it gets one over the ground at a great rate, and without fatigue. Men apparently, are becom- ing tired of assuming boredom, and standing about in limp attitudes at dances, and hostesses are therefore planning their balls with less anxiety than has fallen to their share during several seasons past. The Royal Academy.—From the point of view of the shilling paying public there are as usual, too • many portraits, in this year's Royal Academy. Of late years portraits have become a very prominent feature of the exhibitions, and this season they take up more space than ever. There are five Bailout portraits, including those of Lady Bassoon, Lady Essex and Lady Speyer, and many other eminent portrait painters are also numeroualy represented. Mr. A. S. Cope's painting of the Ring is really a portrait, and is said to be an exceedingly good likeness, but a miniature of Queen Alexandra is all that is to be seen of Her Majesty this year. Apart from Royalty, nearly all the subjects o the various portraits were present at the private view and afforded the opportunity of an interesting com- parison, when standing face to face with themselves on the eanvas. But to those not acquainted with the sitters, these portraits do not present any great attrac- tion. The public in fact, is bored to death with them, and would gladly see the space they take up occupied with more subject pictures, of which there are fewer than usual this year. Apart from this objection, it is generally conceded that merit and talent are repre- sented at this year's Academy without favouritism, and the Chantrey purchases are all of the works of compara- tively less-known artists or so-called "outsiders." Ball Gowns.—Dance dresses should either be built clearing the ground all round or, as the Americans say, with a ' quantity of dry goods " trailing behind. For comfort and convenience, the all-round short skirt is the best of course, after this the trained varieties Are the most easily manipulated, the skirts that rest a few inches on the ground being the most tiresome and

ungraceful, proving very difficult to get hold of, and very inartistic when bunched in the hand. For "little dances" this season, the short skirt will be possible for girls and young matrons, but at balls only the former will don the skirt that clears the ground, trained gowns on such occasions being correct for young and middle-aged matrons alike. The Petal Stole.—This very dainty little neck wrap may be fashioned by the home worker at comparatively small cost, and with very little trouble. First a length of net should be taken, and folded to meet in the middle, the width of the strip being about five inches. Then it must be closely box-pleated, the greatest care being taken to keep the pleats regular. Next, velvet or chenille loops should be sewn to each end,and last of all the chiffon petals which can be bought in various colours and sizes, must be lightly caught to the net here or there as fancy dictates. With dainty summer gowns, such a wrap will prove particularly becoming, and far less costly than the ready made ones to be seen in the shops. Rencivations.—An excellent notion whereby the sleeve of a season or two back can be brought up-to-date, is that of cutting it off a little above the elbow, and slash- ing it up the middle to the shoulder. The severed por- tions can then be connected by means of a lattice work of braid or ribbon of the little " pattes "composed of the material of which the dress is made. Dainty under- sleeves, preferably of lawn, and matching the vest and collar, should be worn with the curtailed sleeves, when the effect will be found to be entirely satisfactory. Combined Kitchen Arrangements.—Many expedients have been resorted to for the solution of the servant problem, which affects not only the housewives of Britain but those of continental countries, and even more of the United States, where the spectacle has been beheld of the "hired girl "sitting down to dinner with the family while the mistress of the house does the waiting. Various experiments have been tried in the States without success and now we read of a scheme in Germany which is being promulgated by a "One- Kitchen House Company. The idea is that in houses which consist of a series of flats, there would be one kitchen under a central mananement, so that the occu- piers of the flats would be relieved from all trouble and anxiety in regard to culinary arrangements. There seems to be something in the project although it will interfere very seriously with the traditions of the Germany family, whose hansfrau prides herself upon her sooking. It is true that the results are not always in conformity with British tastes, but they are eminently satisfactory to those most concerned, and the company will have some natural prejudices to overcome. It will be noticed that the scheme is not generally applicable, but if it is a success there will probably be another for delivering hot dinners at the smaller houses. _ _

Zam4uß Triumpiis Agalit.

Mrs. Scotchford, who lives at 18i; Ilingwood-road, Warnham-road, Waltbamstow, said to a local new4paper

eporter :

"About a year ago I got a tiny scratch on my foot. This became a sore place, and then what seemed to be pimples broke out all over my legs and feeti The irritation was so great that I could not refrain from scratching, with the result that the 'pimples gathered and discharged, making very serious wounds. These wounds grew and spread until my .legs and feet were pitted with great holes.. The pain was awful, and I could not put my feet to the ground. I attended a hospital for several weeks as an out-patient, and was treated for erysipelas and poisoning, but the lotion they gave me never seemed to have the slightest effect. "I then had to go into the Infirmary for three months. After this my " leg was declared to be incurable, and I was dis- charged. I mas at a loss what t 3 do next, when my son's foreman sent me a box of_ Zatn-Buk. I used it, and at once obtained considerable relief. Of course I immediately got some more, and now, after using five boxes, all the wounds are entirely healed. I am as wtil as ever I was in my life, ard back at my old work."

-------- A NEEDED IMPROVEMENT.

A gentleman of considerable influence in Oldham having represented to Mr. Turngull, the chief superin- tendent of the London and North-Western Railway, the wants of would-be visitors and residents to North Wales, has received a very generous response to his application, and we are enabled to state that on and after June Ist next a new express train will leave Man- chester daily for Prestatyn, Rhyl, and Llandudno, at 6.15 p.m., and on Mondays only, commencing June 3rd, a new express train will leave Llandudno at 7.0 a.m. calling at Rhyl and Prestatyn at 7.37 and 7.44 a.m. res pectraely, and Warrington at BAS, arriving in Man- chester at 9.12 a.m. It is further expected that on and after July Ist this train will run daily. In Manchester and Lancashire it is felt that great ,advantage will be taken of the new arrangement, and the action of Mr. Turnbull is much appreciated. Thanks are also due to the gentleman who has taken so much trouble to bring about so desirable a result, which will be of mutual benefit to Lancashire and North Wales. It is well known that both visitors and residents from the Manchester district have in the past been prevented from journeying to North Wales because the outward train service has been too early and the return too late, and it is hoped that this new arrangement will be continued throughout the year, and so place North Wales in a position something approaching that which Blackpool has enjoyed for many years.

GREAT MAY DAY PROCESSION OF HORSES IN

LIVERPOOL.

On Satuday last this annual procession took place in beautiful weather, and as there is no town in the United Kingdom which can boast of so many magnificent horses —more particularly the draught animals used by railway companies, brewers and tradesmen—there is keen com- petition for the various valuable prizes which are offered. Amongst the successful competitors was the celebrated firm of biscuit manufacturers, Messrs. William Crawford and Sons Ltd., of Edinburgh, London and Liverpool, who took the silver cup, value 12 guineas, for the best animal exhibited in a four-wheeled vehicle. The same firm also took the second prize.

PROTECTIVE OVERALL,

With Spring - cleaning looming large in the near future, one of the most useful and necessary additions to one's wardrobe is a big practical overall apron which will completely protect the gown beneath from soil or stain. Just such an apron is pictured in our sketch. The material in which it is achieved is a substantial print, which can be washed and even boiled with im- punity. Over the shoulders comes a neat little yoke, cut square at the top. and coming down

to a point in front. This yoke is lined with the same material, to give it sufficient ffrmness, and is machined all round the edges. On to'the bot- tom of the yoke the pinafore is gathered .with a fair 'amount of fulness,: additional width being secured by the sharp goring of the seams. A wide plain waistband belts in the apron and fastens in front with a big button. The overall is completed by bishop sleeves, which terminate in neat cuffs buttouing at the wrist.

FASHIONABLE FABRICS

Fashion decrees that we are to be most gossa- mer in our 'attire, and the very finest chiffon voile marquisettes and chiffon foulards, together with chiffon satin and taffetas mousseline. will be seen to the exclusion of almost everything else during the coming months. dectlares the Queen. The colourings are simply lovely. The rose-red pinks, the cornflower-blues or that is the veri- table blue they are), the reed-greens, and the lighter tones of the same class of green threaten to be extremely well worn, and nearly, all the thin coloured materials, especially in the red browns and tabac browns, are being made up over white, their charm enhanced by hats with drooping feathers, the 'feathers almost hiding the hat. The skeleton check voiles, with embroidered yokes, make charming blouses, though we are hardly likely to be wearing anything but white blouses this year, made in thick and thin muslin, beautifully embroidered, mostly in satin stitch, though the open. hole embroidery still holds its own. Taffetas and mousseline-de-soie are both very well blended, and the soutache or narrow line of braid is a feature in fashion.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
EMBROIDERED BLOUSE

Quite one of the most noticeable features of up-to-date shirt blouses is the amount of beauti- ful hand-embroidery with which they are deco- rated. The pretty model illustrated in our sketch, though of a very simple type, exhibits this feature in one of its happiest forms. As the embroidery consists entirely of large bold scal- lops, rather coarsely worked, it is very quickly done, and is wonderfully effective. Down the

middle of the front runs a wide box-pleat, bor. dere(' on either edge with the embroidered scallops. On either side of this pleat come three wide tucks, the last projecting over the sleeve, each scalloped at the edge. The sleeves are of the • bishop type, and are taken into deep cuffs buttonholed top and bottom. The original blouse was carried out in pale mauve linen with white embroidery, but any material and any' colour scheme can be successfully employed for the purpose. _ _

FASHION'S FOB MAY.

Taffetas visites, trimmed with fussy little frills of plisse chiffon, and worn in harmony with fine cloth or voile skirts, says Mrs. Jack May in the May World and His Wife, are to ply a leading part this season in the world of dress. Arranged in cape form or with winged sleeves, the fronts resolving into long stole-ends, these " visites " are certain to prove serious rivals to the feather stole, though that they will entirely usurp the place held so long by .these feathery favourites is a very doubtful prophecy on their behalf. The kimono corsage is having a most successful career, and, in spite of the radical revolution it has wrought in silhouette, is being accepted with open arms all along the line. Many variations are rung on the leading theme, such as fichu draperies, bretelles, and the like: but the maid principle of the notion is to be observed through all—namely, a drooping shoulder and wide arm- hole effect.

Satui day, May 11, 1907

i i PEPS WILL GUARD YOU LALL RIGHTS RESERVED. j THE EXPLOITS OF THE ! Against the Manifold Perils of Sudden ONE-EYED MAN. I Weather Changes. 1

By EDWARD TOWLER,

lII.—THE • DETECTIVE'S DISCOMFITURE. To say that the British public was crying aloud against the police for effecting no arrests in connection with the crimes com- mitted at the . Norfolk bank and the bullion robbery, from the specie van of the North- Western • express but feebly expresses the nation's sentiments. The Press and people alike howled for the ,arrest of the gang, for it was felt that such stupendous undertakings .could never be accomplished by a man work- ing single-handed. There must be a master mind, the heads of Scotland Yard were assured, but who was he? Where did lie come from? Secure the leader of the gang, and then there would be no more terrible tragedies. Ah,. to get him! It was a distinct case of catch your hare. There was as yet no clue to the prepetrators of the crime in Norwich, and there was much less evidences to guide them in the affair of the specie van. -So a came about that Inspector Bullen, of Scotland Yard, was de- puted to undertake the unravelling of the mystery.

Inspector Bullen's first step was to journey down to Liverpool, and make the most rigid inquiries. All the information he gained was the fact • that the last carriage had been re- served for two men. Further inquiries went to, confirm the inspector's, impression that there was an American at the bottom of the affair. Point one: An important factor in support of that idea was that before the Eastern Century Bank robbery, -there had been two gentlemen staying at Wpybourne, in Norfolk, under :the names of Rowland T. Rowlands and Henry J. Macassar. Point two: In support -of first theory. The One-eyed Man, on taking the .rooms,, had given a name in the American style, viz., Rowland T. Rowlands, and to the inspector this was an indubitable clue. There- fore, in his own mind, he was satisfied they were Americans.

• His next step was to proceed to Norfolk, and, continuing his inquiries there, he found the cottage at which Rowland T. Rowlands and Henry J. Macassar had stayed; and for *a week Inspector Bullen went "moth hunting" on his own account. One thing he found out from the local coastguard, and that was the sudden appearance and equally sudden dis- appearance of a Submarine off. Cley the day of the bank robbery at Norwich. Could there be any link between the circumstance and the crime? How to connect the two occurrences was the question. From the police he learnt that they had stopped the driver of a Panhard on the road between Cromer and Sheringham on the date of the robbery. Were these—the Panhard and the Submarine—links in the chain of evidence? He felt he was on the track of the miscreants at last. In his own mind he was convinced of the accuracy of his deductions, but yet the affair of the specie van, robbed in mid-country, shewed no possi- bility of a Submarine being used in that con- nection. Stay—yes—was there not the chance of the bullion being in it? Ah ! find the Sub- marine, and he was assured that he would find the bullion. Yes, that was it. The question again rose, find it! Only the coastguard had seen it, and that only for a moment out at sea. Therein lay his greatest difficulty. He was hot-foot on the chase, and his spirits rose as he turned the mystery over and over in his mind during his journey back to London. It was the habit of the inspector to search the agony columns of the papers every morn- ing, and two days after his arrival he saw -the following: "There is no hope unless for- ward the money, as the patient must l take a sea voyage.—Mexico." He cut that out, and determined to follow the correspondence, and next morning's paper contained the following: Have been to sanatorium. Very ill. Some- thing must he done.—Mexico." That also he cut out. Then came the answer: "Meet me Post-office, Charing Cross, 10 p.m. to-night." Inspector Bullen determined to be there also, to see if the correspondents were any of the numerous birds he wanted in his net. He did not for the moment connect them with his Submarine gang. There was no one in particular abottt when he took up his position to watch at 9.50, but promptly on the stroke of ten o'clock two men met by the Post-office entrance. For a moment they conversed in low tones; then the quick ear of the detective caught the words: Still ill." That convinced him he had before him the advertisers in the morning paper. He decided to follow them. They, took the next 'bus to the Bank, riding outside. He took the same 'bus, inside, selecting a seat near the door. The two outside passengers alighted at the Bank, and the inspector followed them to the Metropolitan Railway Station. They booked to Wapping. Inspector Bullen, still un- observed, rode in the guard's van to that station, and then, carefully keeping in the shadows, followed stealthily in the wake of the two men.

It was a long and tedious track, but at last it came to an end by one of the dark and almost deserted docks of the riverside. The men suddenly disappeared, and the next moment Inspector Bullen was seized from be- hind, and his arms pinioned. A bag was thrust over his head, and a stunning blow bereft him of consciousness. When he regained his senses he found he was alone in a cabin of a small yacht, unbound and free, but around him a silence as of death. For some minutes he took in the situation. Then the door opened, and a well-dressed, gentlemanly-lock- ing man entered tht cabin. It was One of the men he had tracked. , "Good evening, Inspector," he said. "I trust your little journey will not inconvenience you."

" Journey !" was the answer. understand you." The other smiled.

"I do not

"You are taking it now,

Inspector." The inspector was mystified, and his face shewed his surprise. The other wont on: " Possibly the slight tap you received two hours ago. Inspector, has somewhat dimmed your usually clear brain. Yob. are down the Thames, near , Gravesend. I believe you came on board at Wapping. The reason you feel no motion and hear no sound is because yeti are in the object of your search, the Sub- marine."

' For a moment even thet phlegmatic calm of the inspector deserted him, and his face shewed the tremendous effect the statement had noon him. The gentleman, no other than the One-eyed Man, scrupulously attired as usual, smiled as he saw the thrust had gone home, for be added, "I take it that it was. the object of your search, Inspector?" The inspector's nerve returned, and he smiled blandly. "You may take it that it v.-as," he answered: "And now that you have found us, or rather, I should say, we have found you, In- spector, have you ' any special plan of action ready? Surely your fertile brain has already conceived some scheme whereby 'we and the ' Submarine are to ,be transplanted, lock, stock, and barrel, shall I say, to New Scot- land Yard?"

Inspector Bullen determined to shew no sign of fear, though he was only too well aware that his life was not worth much in the hands of these men. _ _ _ _

- "I have no objection to so -doing," he an- swered, banteringly, "but at the .moment I must confess that I see no way." The One-eyed Man answered tartly. "No. You see no way, Inspector; you see no way. For the second time to-night you are blind. We do not 'intend you shall see a way either. The advertisement was well planned, In- pector, well planned; but we were afraid you might ' tumble ' and not follow our decoy. How was it your astute brain saw no connec- tion between the advertisements and us, eh, Mr. Inspector? for, personally, I do not„.think you connected it with us." Inspector Bullen replied simply, "I did not. It is my habit to study 'advertisements-L. it is my business, and seeing yours in the Eqiltrse of that business-, was on hand at your cer.dezvous." •

_ . The One-eyed Man sneered. "You saw nc connection. You are not as sharp as we _gave you credit for. It was signed 'Mexico.' Does Oat convey anything tO you?" " Mexico? " said the inspector. "Mexico?" "Yes, Mexico," again sneered the One-eyed Man. "Are you still blind, air!. _

For the moment the brain of the inspectoz was working under forced draught. Then he knew. Like a flash it passed through his mind. "Good heavens!' he exclaimed, involun•

tarily. "So, Mr. Inspector, you see now. My lieutenant is known as the 'Greaser.' You were aware of that. Now, ' Greaser ' is essen- tially a Mexican word; that was why we used it to lure you here. What we were afraid of was that you might have the decoys arrested as suspicious persons. There again, if I may teach you your duty, you missed your point. On the other hand, seeing no connection, you could not take it. Naturally. Really, In- spector, I fail to see how you have achieved ur present standing in Scotland Yard. You a.re no Sherlock Holmes, Mr. Bullen." The inspector winced. "You may maxe what remarks you like," he replied. "You are not yet out of the wood." "Nor are you out of the Submarine. Per- sonally, I do not think you will be for some time, and when you are it will be in such a state that we are assured you cannot pry into any further secrets of ours." You threaten Me ? " said the inspector. threaten! Pardon me, I believe in the old adage, 'Threatened men live long', and I never threaten, Mr. Bullen. I perform. You hear me? I perform." The inspector looked him fearlessly in the face. "Your record bears you out for -the murderer that you are," he answered. He was determined to shew no signs of fear to this man. The One-eyed Man smiled. "You have a good memory," he said. "I have had many aliases, but that of murderer has not been applied before." That may be. Your victims never live to accuse 'you,' answered the inspector._ _ _

The One-eyed Man shrugged- his shoulders. "Well hit," he said. I acknowledge the point—they do not." "May I dtsk your intentions as regards my- self ? " inquired the inspector.' "Certainly. We take you out to sea, lash you to our little platform by the conning tower, and " "And what?" hoarsely asked the other. "And 'sink—after a time," was the cold, brutal reply. Inspector Bullen's face paled, but it was not from fear. In the innumerable tight cor- ners in which he had been he had had many a rough-and-tumble fight. One was a fight in hot blood, when knives flashed like a glint of sunlight, and a bullet pinged against a wall like the hum of a bee. Re knew no fear, but what palgd his cheeks now was the hor- rible, slow, relentless death that he pictured —a death where the agony was prolonged possibly for hours. "Sink—after a time." After a time—what length of time? Then hie eyes lit with hope. There was one chance, a passing' ship might see him before the boat sank, for would he net be the only one upon the fatal platform of the conning tower? The One-eyed Man noticed the renewed light in the inspector's eyes, and said: "You. think you may be seen and rescued, Mr. Bullen? You must have, a poor opinion of our methods of business. We have a peri- scope, and, although we are underneath the waves, the surface of the sea is seen by us, as in the camera obscura. You follow met"

The heart of the •inspector died within him. "I follow," he said, simply. The One-eyed Man went on, "It is usual for a Submarine to stay below the water for four or five hours only. You have been below - for nearly that period. But our boat differs , from all others in that we can remain below for a month, if necessary. So, you see, there is no occasion for us to hurry. The special

invehtion is our own. By the way, what would it be worth to your Government, Mr. Bullen ? Wouldn't it be cheap at a million, or cheap -at my .pardon? Possibly I may offer it when I get in the 'corner you are in. Who knows? The pitcher that goes—' You know the rest. You are fond of proverbs, Mr. Inspector." "'Gets, broken at last Mr. Rowland T. - Rowlands," said the inspector. The One-eyed Man laughed that wintry laugh of his—the laugh that preceded the death of the two men in the Euston-road. "Hit again. Inspector, hit again. So you tumbled to that. Well, well, ,upon my: word, you are really smart. You can follow, you and your cursed police. That is all you can do. Follow, follow like a flock of sheep, but they never catch the master; they never • have brains for that., Immediately they scent some- thing they rush off to the papers, and blazon forth the fact that they have got a clue. That is all they will ever get of mine. Let them follow it. I wish you good-night; you may sleep on the cushions. In the morning .you will be ,off Harwich, then we will see ,to you." With that he left the cabin. tiispector Bullen's eyes travelled round and round the cabin. There was absolutely no escape for him. It was not like a ship. Had it been he might have rushed upon deck and sprung overboard, trusting to Providence. But here it was a • prison indeed, for water was round and above him. He got up and tried the door. The moment his hand touched ; the handle a bell rang out loudly. He started back, astounded. A moment after a man entered.

" It'ou rang," he said; "what do you re- quire?

"Supper," said the inspector, brusquely. ",Do you intend to starve me?" And he scanned the face of the man to see if he knew him; but he did not " Good ! I will bring it," answered the man. "You have a good heart, Mr. Police- man." •

The inspector smiled. "So you know my fate, do you?" he queried. "I do," was the reply. "We all do here. No one who Comes into this web escapes." "Is there a hope for me? " asked the in- spector. "If you could help me I would make it: worth your while." . The man smiled. "The price?" he asked. "The ones?" " How-- would a thousand pounds do, and pardon, supposing we captured this craft and Lis owners? " he whispered. ,The man grinned. " A thousand pounds ! Great Scott! a man could do a. lot on that." " Yes," said the inspector, "he could. I would guarantee you. that amount and a par- don."

The man grinned again. ".My s'ilare of the boodle on this craft, is fifteen th.iusand pounds. Am I giving it to you for r. rotten thousand pounds and a pardon?. No fear. The one point about our One-eyed Boss i serve him well and he'll pay well ; serve him on the cross, and—well, he puts us safe beyond the . temptation of a miserable police reward. • No, thanks, Mr. Inspector; no, thanks. Now get your supper." And he went aWay. The heart of the inspector died within him. He was without hope. The supper was brought in; and he made a pretence of eat- ing it, revolving schemes the while, but all to no purpose; and at last, -utterly worn ,out, he ; fell into a troubled sleep. He was awakened some hours later by the " opening of the cabin dooli. Six men en- ' tered, and almost before he was aware of their presence -seized him, bound his arms and legs, and carried him on deck, where in the bright sunshine the One-eyed Man stood, while tiny waves rippled against the now rnlcss Submarine. 1 sow," he said,. "your hour has. come. Inspector made no reply. "Tie him up," he ordered. " Stay ! Let us make him look as -if he had had a chance. Rig a life-belt on him, so that when we cut him adrift it will look the better. We must study appearances, he said, cynically. It was done. "Lash 'him to. the flagstaff," he com- manded. This was also done. " Below !" he said, laconically, and the crew crept silently through the manhole of the boat and went below. The One-eyed Man followed, saying as he did so: "Au revoir, Mr. 'Millen, au revoir." ' The cover was screwed down from inside, .Ind the inspector- we,s left face to face with death. How long would it be before they sank him—drowned him like a dog—how long? The cold sweat ran down his Ince. Dyer the sea a, white mist crept as, a curtain, nearer and nearer, and the little craft was enveloped in it. Still they kept. above; • must have been nearly half-an-hour sin.• .hey left him. To the inspector it was a ifetime. Alt: what was that? They were sinking. Yes. the deck, once two feet from Ihe sea, was now level with it. Ho watched 'he rivets in the plating.. No, tbey had stopped again. It was the diabolical torinp

of the One-eyed Man. - He felt Ins °rain. bursting, and then out of the fog loomed the bow of a steamer. There was a stunning crash, and he knew no more until he found himself floating upon the water, his cork jacket supporting him admirably. An hour later he .was picked up by a Lowestoft steam -- drifter.

He thought of the Submarine. It had gone down with all its .booty and its crew. It was lost for ever, together with the greatest gang .of ruffians that ever enineered a crime. • But had it?

The steamer that ran them down only tore away the little tower and its flagstaff, for the Submarine's owners, profiting by the lesson of the ill-fated Al, had a double deck below. the conning-tower. So all the evil effect felt by them was a tremendous /shock and a boat .that almost turned turtle, and then—quiet- And the Submarine was still a tremendous power for evil. But that Inspector Bul had still .to learn.

APPLETON RIFLE CLUB.

Annual Meeting.

The annual meeting of the Apple-ion Rifle Club was held at the Range on Saturday aftemoon under the presidency of Mr. Seger Owen.

FINANCIAL.

The financial statement showed that the only liability of the Club was a bank overdraft of £29 9s. 11d, as against £52 10s. 10d. outstanding accounts on 31st December 1905, and that the balance surplus on the revenua account was £lB 3s. Gd. as compared With £14.05. 11d, at the end of 1905.

The balance sheet was passed. SUBSCRIPTION REDUCED.

The meeting Approved alterations of rules, the effect being to give the committee power to add to their number and to reduce the annual subscription to ss. It was stated that 23 new members had been elected provisionally upon the subscription being thus reduced.

SECRETARY'S REPORT.

In his report the Secretary (Mr. _Arthur Down) said: —At the end of the 1905 season the nominal strength of "the club was 90 members, but the club as constituted at the end of the 1006 season consisted of 33 members, ten of whom were members of local battalions of the Volunteers. One member has since resigned. The apparent falling off of membership was due to the fact that, under a resolution carried at the annual general meeting, held 12th May, 1906, no outstanding subscrip- tions prior to 1905 have been asked for. This, together with resigna tions,caused the reduction in the membership All arrears,of subscriptions for 1905 and 1906 have been paid by the present members. Fifteen members attended the range during last season. Fourteen competed for the" Rifleman's Certificate," and there certificates.were awarded, two to Lance-Sergeant W. Stewart and one to Mr. A. Down. The competitions for these certificates aroused much interest, and greatly improved the shoot- ing. Seven members" competed for the Donegall Bronze Badge—provided annually by the National Rifle Associa- tion—the winner being Sergeant-Drummer J. W. Plinston, who, on the 22nd of September last, made a score of 78 opt of a possible 80 points. Three thousand three hundred and ninety-five cartridges of various kinds were used by the club members last season. The Warrington Volunteers had the use of the range last season (as previously) on three evenings in each week, and also for their Easter competitions. The Warring- ton branch of the National Service League also used the range on nine Thursday afternoons, and held their first annual competition there on Saturday, the 29th of September, the club allowing them on that day the - exclmive uss of the range. The rent paid by the National Service League and the proceeds of the sale of ammunition to them has resulted in a small profit to the club. The debt on the initial expenditure on the equipment of the range has been paid by an overdraft on the bank, and the committee expect that by the end of the present year the club will be almost, if not .quite, out of debt. For the coming season arrangements have been made with the colonel and officers of the Warrington Volunteers for the battalion to have the use of the range on Monday, Tuesday, and Friday evenings, as before. Satisfactory terms have also been arranged with the executive of the Warrington branch of the National Service League for the League to have the ese of the range on 11 dates during the ,60tning.season. The rapid growth of the National Service League in the Warrington district has caused a much more extensive use of the range by them than was the case at the beginning of last season, necessitating the making of different arrangements for their convenience this year. Mutually satisfactory terms have been made with the executive of the Stockton Heath Company of the Church Lads' Brigade for those who are 16 years or over to receive histruction in rifle shooting at the range on ten Wednesday evenings, from 6.30 to 8.30. I regret to have to inform you that Mr. Edward Bate, who has been our caretaker ever since the formation of the club in the year 1902, is unsible to undertake the duties this season owing to failing health. The services of Mr. Isaac Broady have, however, been secured, and judging from the way he has taken up his duties, I feel sure we shall have nothing to complain of. The clubseason this year will be from the 4th' of May to the 28th of Sep- tember, and the range will be open to club members on 21. Saturdays, from 3 p.m. to 5.30 p.m., and on 21 Thurs- days, from 5 p.m. to 7 p.m., when a marker and the caretaker (Mr. Broady) will be in attendance. There will be five monthly club prize competitions on the last Saturdays in the several months,and the committee hope that on the remaining shooting days all marksmen who are sufficiently competent will compete for the "Ekilled Shot" and" Rifleman's Certificates." A special target will be Set apart each day for the use of those who are not sufficiently experienced to compete for the certifi- cates. Two War Office miniature rifles have been pre- sented to the club for use this and subsequent seasons, one by Mr. V. B. Davies and the other by the Secre- tary; and the three Martini-Francott rifles belonging to the club have been thoroughly overhauled and repaired. The Committee hope that others may join the club, and that full advantage may be taken of the facilities for shooting, both on Thursdays and Saturdays, which are offered to the members of the club. In conclusion, I should like to mention that Mr. Thomas Daintith and his sons have rendered valuable service to the club by kindly allowing a notice to be displayed in their shop, and by obtaining the names of gentlemen desirous of becoming members. -

The S;cretary was warmly complimented upon the report which was adopted.

OFFICERS.

Mr. T. H. Lyon was re-elected president; Sir Gilbert Greenall,' Bart., and Col. Ridgway. V.D., vice-presi- dents; and the following constitute the committee : The Rev. H. A. Lester, and Messrs. V. B. Davies, A. Warburton, .1. J. Whitley, Segar Owen, P. J. Grocott, R. M. Roberts, E. Draper, F. Warburton, .1. Hobin, F. Gandy, T. A. Sutton, and J. Wilcock; bon treasurer, Mr. L. A. Voisey ; hon. secretary, Mi. A. Down; and armourer, Mr. T. Daintith.

EMMANUEL CHURCH.

.iV=MM

!!H-FORMtA" -STRENGTHENER

===== 0002601_19070511_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
Iwl.to rebuilt the instrument to give them a recital. He believed Emmanuel Church might take credit to itself in the fact that Mr. Rushworth had not visited Warrington before in any capacity, and they were glad to have the distinction of being the first Church in which he gave an organ recital ill the good old town. They were most anxious to pay the bill for the work which had been so well done, and they believed that under God's blessing they would be able to do so almost immediately. They had hoped to have the presence of the Rev. J. Manning, the resident minister of Bold-street, but unfortunately he was detained on Synod work in Liverpool. Mr. W. Hickman was, how- ever, present to represent the trustees, through whose kindness they had been able to secure the instrument. Mr. W. HICKMAN said he was in cordial sympathy with the object of that gathering and with the good work which they were endeavouring to do in connec- tion with that house of prayer. That was not the first time he had visited the Emmanuel congregation. The Bold-street trustees had the kindliest feelings towards them and wished them every success. He was sure that feeling was reciprocated by the Emmanuel congregation. It was very fitting that the organ should stay in Warrington and still more fitting that it should remain in Bold-street--in fact it would have been quite a tragedy to take it anywhere else. He trusted that the Emmanuel Church would become a greater power in the town and that the members would live long to listen to the beautiful notes of the organ. The recital was then given and proved highly enjoy- able. In addition to demonstrating the great ability of the organist, it brought out to the full the power of the organ and all were charmed with its sweet notes. The following was the programme :—Polonaise, Chopin; Air, Handel • Prelude and Fugue,Bach; Andante, Smart; Offertoire, Batiste; Melody, Rea ; Festal March, Best. The most creditable selections were undoubtedly the "Prelude and Fugue " and the" Offertoire " in which T..‘ Mr. Rushworth showed a fine nom and of technique combined with very tasteful expres ion. In addition to the organ recital, there were vocal olos by Mrs. A. Booth and Mr. Levi Richardson. M • Booth's sweet and powerful voice was heard to great advantage in "God who madest earth and heaven" and "Hear ye Israel "which were most artistically interpreted. Mr. Richardson gave very feeling renderings of "Where 'ere von walk (Handel) and "Conie unto Me" (Coenen), the latter being particularly effective. A collection was made on behalf of the organ fund and realised £l3.

AS WE LIKE IT.

The Greek eats currants raw, ivith his bread, but we in Britain adapt the fruit to our national tastes, and every year we eat many millions of pounds of currants simply cooked in bread. The King's physician strongly recommends this plain, wholesome food, and every enterprising baker supplies a nice light loaf, liberally sprinkled with fruit, at a price only slightly in advance of that of ordinary plain bread.

The following letter from the Warrington Master Builders' Association was read at a meeting of the Paving and Sewerage Committee :—At a meeting of this association held on the 13th inst. I was instructed to write you on behalf of the association, respectfully requesting you that in all future quantities you should include a commission of 5 per cent. on all lump sum amounts included in the quantities, except lump sums for contingencies, and that the followimg words at pre- sent appearing in the quantities "add builders' profit," Ste., be eliminated. I shall be much obliged if you will give this matter your consideration, and let me know whether you can see your way to meet the builders in their request.—lt was resolved that the cpmmittee can- not accept the proposals above contained. Alderman TINNTON moved the confirmation of the minutes, and said the Builders' 4asociationshad a perfect right to look after their interests,but when they clashed with the general interests of the town it was the duty of the committee to stand up and defend the town. (Rear, hear.) The present was not the only occltsion on which the association had endeavoured to force the hand of the committee. The minutes were approved. WARRINGTON AND THR SHIP CANAL. NEW CLAUSE AGREED TO. At a meeting of the Rivers Committee complaints having been received from several manufacterers of the discharge of salt water into the river b 5) means of the sluices at Walton Lock and requesting the Corporation to take immediate action, it was resolved, "That the matter be left in the hands of the Chairman and the Town Clerk." The TOWN CLERK submitted the clause which had been agreed upon with the Manchester Ship Canal Company for the protection of the Corporation of Warrington and of traders, manufacturers and others carrying on business at or near Warrington. The minutes were confirmed. a_ SECONDARY SCHOOL AND THE GYMNASIUM. The Gymnasium Committee resolved that at the request of the School Management Committee, this committee meet their sub-committee on Wednesday, ' the Bth of May, at seven o'clock, for the purpose of dis- cussing the question of the use of the Gymnasium by the Secondary School scholars as an assembly and examination room. Alderman TIN. NioN proposed the adoption of the minutes. He said if anything could be done to utilise the Gymnasium for the benefit of the Secondary School they as a committee were quite prepared to give fair consideration to any proposal the School Management Sub-committee might might make. At present there was not adequate accommodation in the Secondary School for the scholars, and if the Committees could not come to terms about the matter it was more than probable considerable expense would have to be gone to by the Educationi Committee to find room for exami- nations and for an assembly. Councillor CLEGG seconded, and the minutes were adopted.

they were doing something that vas not safe. (Hear, hear.) Mr. Warren announced that invitations to dine had been received from Sir Gilbert Greenell for Friday, May 17th, and Mr. Crosfield for Saturday, May 18th. (Applause.) PRAISE FOR THE PLAYERS. Mr. AsHTON submitted the toast of the Warrington team. They had, he said, achieved a most exalted honour, and one that had been contested for by all the principal clubs in the four shires. One feature that particularly characterized the play of the Warrington team had been their absolute fairness. He was certain that the greatest sceptic that ever watched the Northern Union game—and there were plenty who took great exception to a professional game of that char- acter—could not take exception to their methods or style of play. It had evoked from people he had spoken to very warm-hearted praise, and their conduct on the field had shown to the football public that they were able to play the game without any degree of roughness. Everyone must feel that the sacrifices made by the team as a body had helped materially to their success. Both the Press and the public had spoken highly of the manner in which the team went upon the field of play. To turn out at night to train,after having put in a day's work, was a great sacrifice, and he hoped the younger players would take that as an example of what per- severance and strict attention to duty rendered possible. Mr. Ashton coupled with the toast the name of the captain, J. Fish. The toast was heartily drunk and in responding the captain said he was proud to captain such a team as the Warrington Club now possessed, and of the fact that they had again secured the trophy. He hoped they would all keep together next year, and in that case they had a chance of winning the cup again. (Hear, hear.) Mr. A. NA.YLOR in a brief speech praised the work of the trainers of the team. They had worked exceedingly hard, and deserved a medal, or some special recognition from the committee. Mr. D. ISHERWOOD thanked the committee of the club for the generous manner in which they had treated the team during the training and preliminary cup matches. They had done their best for all the players, and the players had noticed this, and put every effort forward. He wished the committee success, and hoped they would be appointed again for next year. Mr. WALLINGTON, an auditor, congratulated both the team and the committee on their success. He supposed that as a Lancashire Club they must take credit to themselves in having established a record by twice winning the cup, and their was no doubt the team had upheld the traditions of good old Warrington as a sporting town. (Hear, hear.) The PRESIDENT observed that they had had very little trouble with their players during the past season. He was proud to hear Mr. Isherwood compliment the com- mittee for their treatment of the players. He believed Warrington was noted all over England for the way they treated their players; when they promised a thing they did it. He would like to hear a few words from Mr. Tilley. When they got that player from Widnes he (the President) confessed they did not think they had such a great,man as he had turned out. They had another good full-back an Berg, one of the best, in his (the Presi- dent's) opinion in the country. Unfortunately, Berry was injured, and they had the good fortune to get Mr. Tilley. At first he (the President) thought they had made a mistake in the big amount of money they paid the Widnes Club, although they wanted to benefit that organization, but Tilley had turned out to be worth double what they paid Widnes and the wages they paid Tilley as a professional player. (Hear, hear). Mr. TILLEY said he never regretted coming to War- rington, although his love was with his old team at Widnes, and he was sure Warrington would get the best out of him. No two members of the Warrington team were prouder than Taylor and himself, because they were about the youngest playing members, and it was the first time they had ever won a medal. They had played long and strongly in trying to get one and had been within a match of it, but that was the first time they had ever passed through the final, and they would value their medals far more than their intrinsic worth. Plenty of people thouaht Oldham were the better team, because they were pressing for 50 minutes; but a team that could only penetrate their opponents' defence once and allowed their opponents to score 17 points, was surely not the better. The better team was the one whose defence had proved more powerful than the other side's attack, and he thought Warrington deserved the cup. He hoped Warrington would be successful for many years to come. Mr. S. LEES said he had pleasure in, supporting Mr. Isherwood's remarks about the treatment received from the committee, and he thanked them for their kindness. If all players were treated as they were in Warrington it would be much better for the Union. Mr. EfAcKErr, one of the trainers, said they and the players had always got on well together;and he hoped they would do even better in the future. The PRESIDENT said they were proud of their Welsh players. He regarded Jenkins as one of the finest "catches" they ever made. Shugars was also another fine player they got from Wales. Mr. JENKINS said he had much pleasure in coming to

NEW CAR-TOP COVERS.

The TOWN CLERK reported to a meeting of the Electricity and Tramways Committee that the Board of Trade had sanctioned the borrowing of £5OO for car-top covers. It wAs resolved that teaderefor six car-top covers be adviVtised for. ' The MAYOR proposed and Councillor W*KEsTSON seconded, the adoption of the minutes.

TRAMWAY MANAGER'S SALARY. The Electricity and Tramways Committee recom- mended that the salary of Mr. T. Teller be increased by the sum of £25 per annum. Councillor LORD thought some explanation should be given before the Council was asked to confirm the resolution. He understood that in June, 1905, Mr. Telfer's salary was raised from £l3O to £l5O per annum, which was contrary to the terms of his engagement, which stated that his salary was to inorease at the rate of £lO a year. He did not know whether Mr. Telfer had increased duties to perform,whether he had renderei exceptional service, or whether there was any extra- ordinary success in connection with the tramways department. _ - - - -

Councillor BURTON thought these increases in salary were coming rather too quickly, and that Mr. Telfer might wait a little longer until the lines were extended. Mr. Telfer had been dealt with more liberally than his engagement stipulated. Councillor CHANTLER asked if Mr.Telfer had applied for the increase.

The MAYOR said Mr. Telfer applied.through him to the committee. Mr. Telfer took into consideration the amount of profit he had made, that he was the worst paid tramways manager in England, and that he was expecting when the balance sheet came out there would be something like £l,OOO to £1,600 clear profit. There were eight members present at the committee meeting, and with the exception of one the resolution was passed unanimously. Councillor AsircuorTsaid it seemed as though all the profits had to go to the officials, according to the Mayor's suggestion. Alderman BOLTON said the time to raise Mr. Telfer's salary would be when the increased profits were laid I before them. The maximum of £l5O had been grantedl before the stipulated time, because the committee I thought he did his work so well. The present applica- tion was inopportune, and he proposed it be referred back. Councillor ASHCROFT seconded. , Alderman FRANCOMB said he did not like to hcar man saying he was the worst paid official in England. He did not suppose Mr. Telfer would stay here a week if he could get a better salat7 somewhere else. It would be wrong to give him an increase on the assump- tion that he was going to make a profit. Considering that he was only advanced in June 1905, he might have waited until be had shown some extraordinary results, I and then he (the speaker). did not think the Council 'would treat him in a niggardly way. Dr. JOSZPH pointed out that it was not Mr. Telfer who said he was the s orst paid official. That was a misunderstanding. He simply submitted a tsble from which the committee deduced the fact.

ILLUMINATED CAR PROCESSION.

A striking spectacle was afforded the townspeople on ' Tuesday evening when the victory was further cele- -Ibrated by an illuminated oar procession. The decora- tions of tbe car containing the team and officials with [the precious trophy, probably surpassed in brilliance any previous display of the kind. No fewer than 850 coloured lamps were used for the illuminations, so that viewed from a distance the car appeared to be one mass of gorgeous and sparkling colour. In a prominent position the long-to-be-remembered years of " 1905 " and "1907" were picked out ip dazzling lights, and Union Jacks and artificial 'evergreens, tastefully arranged, considerably heightened the effect. The interior of the oar along with the handrails were beauti; fully embellished with the familiar "primrose and blue." The whole of the decorative work reflected great credit upon head-fitter Hindle and his staff. The flags had been lent by Mr. J. S. Garland and the evergreens by Mr. W. A. Leslie. Shortly after eight o'clock a start was made from the tram sheds in Mersey- street, and all along the route scenes of great enthusiasm were witnessed. Preceding the illuminated car was a tram containing the Volunteer Band who, under the conductorship of Bandmaster Richardson, discoursed triumphal music. Hearty cheers went up when members of the victorious team were seen seated in the illuminated car and the cup, which was placed in a conspicuous position was much admired. The oar was in charge of Edward Widdocks and Conductor Arthur Ellis, while Inspector Quinn superintended the procession. The band car was driven by John Howarth and Conductor Timperley was in charge. The pro- cession occupied about three hours' the whole of the routes being traversed. On leaving the shed the cars proceeded to Rylands-street and then on to Victoria- square, Stockton Heath. A return through the town was made, and the car journeyed to Sankey Bridges. From here a visit was made to Latchford. The next run was to Longford, and from there a return was made to the Cemetery and back to the club's headquarters.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES

Baptist Church, Golborne-street.

The Rev. Edward Moore, of Leeds, who was formerly a pastor at the church, conducted the services at the Baptist Church on Sunday. Large congregations, is- eluding many old friends of the rev, gentleman, assembled and his discourses were listened to with interest and pleasure.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
Wycliffe P.S.A.

There was a good attendance at the meeting of the Wyeliffe P.S.A. Society, held on Sunday under the presidency of Mr. G. Smiley. Ur. Bennett, of Ashton- under-Lyne, delivered an address on " The Talents." He said that God was the great Giver of the talents. The use or misuse of the talents was what they were judged by. No matter what the gifts of others might be, eloquence or brilliancy, they did not make any difference to their own talents.lt was their use of the one,two or five talents they possessed that they should have to account for. In a town he (the speaker) knew, there was a pro- nounced infidel who was sick unto death. No one would dare to take flowers to him, but a little girl did so. The infidel received the flowers, which touched his heart, and the incident made him think differently and brought him to Christ. That was God's loving way of dealing with him. Mr. Payton rendered an organ solo. Mr. D. Owen also took pact in the meeting. Three new members were heartily welcomed, making a total of 359. Mr. W. Beswick at the close expressed the pleasure of the brethren in seeing Mr. If arrar back again after a severe accident.

Presentation to P.C. Hardy. On Monday evening, P.C. Hardy, who will shortly retire from the Warrington Police Force after 26 years service, was the recipient of a token of the gond wishes of the inhabitants of the Latchford district amongst whom he had been stationed for seven years. The proceedings took place at the Black Bear Inn, Knutsford-road. Mr. R. T. Bridge, on behalf of the subscribers asked Mr. Hardy to accept a purse of Fold (£10) as a token of the esteem and regard entertained for him,'and in recognition of his faithful service in the district. He hoped Mr. Hardy. would long enjoy his well-earned retirement allowance. (Applause) In reply Mr. Hardy expressed his thanks for that expression of their appreciation of his services. His duty at times might have been unpleasant; but, so far as he was concerned, he did not think he had ever shirked it, at least not knowingly. A hearty vote of thanks was accorded to the subscribers, and also to the Chairman.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
REV. THOMAS LAW IN AMERICA.

Rev. Thomas Law has received a cordial welcome on his brief visit to the United States. At the first meet- ing held at New York, some hundreds of ministers were present from the city and district. Dr. Anson Atterbury, a prominent Presbyterian leader presided, and Mr. Mcßee; Editor of " The Churchman " responded to Mr. Law's speech. At Providence, Mr. Law met Gipsy Smith, and a meeting was held attended by a thousand ministers and others. A similar gathering was held at Boston, on each occasion a publio dinner following.— Mr. Bryce, the British Ambassador, has promised to present Mr. Law to the President of the United States.

PRIMITIVE METHODIST CENTENARY.

A BRIEF HISTORY.

Rise of the Denomination.

The history- of Primitive Methodism proper really dates from 1810. It is necessary, however, to go back to the very &ginning of the nineteenth century in order to show what led to the birth of this new Church. A few words ought to be written at the out- set about the condition of England iu the year 1800, for that condition forms a fitting introduction to our theme. Those who are fond of talking about the good old clays and comparing past with present to the detriment of the present will find it necessary to change their tune when the real facts are known, for to go back a hundred years, even in English history, • is almost to go back to pre-civilized days. What is • known to us as the "Black country" (where Primitive Methodism had its rise) was noted for the rough and ! rude manners of the populace. It • was a risky busi- ness for a stranger to venture through some of the • villages of that day. His very appearance was enough to raise the cry "heave half a brick at him," ; or to tempt the dog-fancier to let his favourite bull- dog try its teeth on the stranger's legs. Turning to the I mining districts of S.E. Durham we learn that here. I cock-fighting was a favourite amusement, Sabbath- desecration was an unblushing evil, and wakes and fairs were held on the Lord's Day. Pugilism, drunken- ness, and smuggling were rife. Profane swearing was the habit not only of puddlers and pitmen, but of squires and country magnates alike. The miner of those days was treated little better than a serf. Strikes were not infrequent, blood was shed and men were shot in connection therewith. And the very worst fact that we have to record is the fact that many of the parish der y,instead of mending the manners and morals of their neighbourhoods, sank far towards the level of those whom they should have helped to raise. The district in and around Mow Cop in North Staffordshire (the birthplace of the denomination) was as dark a spot as could be found in all England. The biographer of one of the founders of the Church records his opinion that the Kidsgrove and Harrisea- head colliers were quite as ignorant and debased as the Kingswood colliers of Wesley's and Whitfield's day. The whole district was lying in wickedness. Drunkenness, cock-fighting, bull-baiting, poaching, pugilism, midnight marauding and profanity made this part of the country like a territory of heathendom. With the exception of one small chapel-of-ease there was no place of worship to be seen for miles on the Staffordshire side of Mow Cop. ; Now it came to pass in the year 1800 that business affairs brought a young man by the name of Hugh Bourne very frequently into the neighbourhood of Mow. He was now 28 years of age, and his conver- sion had taken place the year previously. His home had been a moorland farm. One of his memorials says: "As a residence there could hardly be any- thing more bleak, desolate and lonely. In the whole neighbourhood there were only some 2 or 3 other houses, and beyond these was the wide stretch of the moor. There was no road, public or private, not even a footroad to the house or anywhere near it, and to complete the isolation and loneliness, the only access to the house was over a wide brook upon a plank, and I often for weeks together the family saw no one beyond their own circle, and of the great outside world they I knew but very little." Hugh Bourne was very much like his environment. He had a strong, rugged,nature marked however by a most painful bashfulness and reticence. He was constitutionally shy and serious. If he had not possessed wonderful reserves of dogged- ness and courage he would have remained for ever unknown. His father was a drnnken sot, a derider of Methodism and dissent, and yet, a stiff Churchman. His mother early taught her boy to fear God and walk righteously though she had no ability to lead him to the joys of the Christian life. Bourne's childhood was a sad one. There is a sense in which he never was a boy. *Oh that I had had someone to take me by the hand and instruct me in the mystery of faith and the nature of a free, full, and present salvation," he after- wards wrote, "Row happy it would have been for me! But like Bunyan's Pilgrim, I had to make my way alone. The Lord neither gave me guide nor com- panion in the way to the Cross. I was painfully con- vinced of sin when I was but a small boy and this Iwithout the aid or knowledge of any man, and during my 20 sorrowful years I went through much moral and religious readings." This is what made him so concerned about the children in the after days. In a book borrowed from a Methodist neighbour he found quite a library. Within the covers of that one book were the Life of Fletcher, of Madeley, Jane Cooper's 1" Letters," the lives of one or two early Methodist preachers, Alleine's "Alarm to the unconverted" and a treatise on the articles and homilies of the Church of England. A sermon of Wesley's on the Trinity revealed much to him and certain Quaker literature also proved very fascin- ating to the serious young man. "I was much edified "he writes, "I was able to see a little more clearly into the mystery and power of faith." But it was John Fletcher who was used by God to bring to an end the anxious gloom of 20 years. "One Sunday , morning" he writes "in my father's house at Bemersley I sat reading in Mr. Fletcher's 'Letters on the Spiritual manifestation of the Son of God,' and realised the blessing named in John xiv. 21, and I was born again in an instant! Yea, I passed from death unto life. In an instant I had power over sin which I had not before, and I was filled with joy and love and glory which made a full amends for the 20 years suffering. The Bible looked new; creation looked new • and I felt a love to all mankind; and my desire was that friends and enemies and all the world, if possible, might be saved." This was in 1799. And it was while in this mood that his business brought him in 1880 to the neighbourhood of Mow Cop (To be coxtinued).

WARRINGTON MAN'S SUICIDE.

A sad case of suicide was inquired into at the , Warrington Coroni3r's Court on Tuesday when Mr. F. A. Jones', deputy coroner, held an inquest on the body of Bichard Slater (66), 31, Helsby-street, Warrington. Alice Haslam,deceased's daughter, stated that she last saw her father alive at nine o'clock on Friday morning when he said he would go out to look for work. For some time he had suffered from liver complaint and had on many occasions been attended by Dr. Martin. IDuring the past few weeks he had been very depressed but witness had never heard him threaten to take away his life. The fact that be had not been working lately had preyed o' nhis And. —John Stafford, boatman, Church View, Hollins Green, said that at 2.15 on Friday afternoon he was going home along the towing path of the Bridgewater Canal at Woolston when he saw a man jump into the , water. Witness was 300 yards away from him at the time, and when he hurried to the spot deceased came to the side. Witness helped him out of the water, and whilst he was doing so deceased said "Mind my back." Deceased was left on the bank while witness went for stimulants, and when he was about twenty yards away deceased again entered the water and swam towards the opposite bank, but sank.—P.C. jowett gave evidence.—The Deputy-Coroner said he could understand a man committing suicide in the ' fit of depression caused by liver trouble.—A verdict of "Suicide whilst of unsound mind" was returned.

MRS. D'OYLY CARTE'S GIFT,

Mrs. d'Oyly Carte has sent a letter and a cheque for £25 to the secretary of the Middlesbrough Amateur Operatic Society, who had intended to perform " The Mikado "this week in aid of local charities and were obliged to substitute" The Yeomen of the Guard." The letter expresses regret at the disappointment the Society and the public suffered, and the .-C25 is sent as a subscription to the Middlesbrough charity funds.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0036.txt | included=true | included =====
MOTOR OUTSTRIPS A TRAIN.

A motor-car is this week completing a journey of 1,543 miles with the object of demonstrating the advantages of the motor over the railway. It purposes to arrive at given places ahead of the train, and on its first run from Carlisle to Cockermouth on Tuesday it easily beat the locomotive.

GIRL'S THEFT.

On Tuesday at Present Sessions, Emily Bruce, servant, Widnes, was charged with stealing three gold rings the property of Mrs. Elizabeth Knowles, Rainhill, and was bound over under the First Offenders Act.

THE EXAMINER;

MAY 11th. 1907

There was nothing in the TI:IE IRISH BILL. least revolutionary in the Bill introduced to the nuse of Commons on Tuesday by Mr. BIRRELL. "Q the contrary, it is a measure which must fall Short of the expectations of almost all parties in parliament. Perhaps Lord DUNRA.VEN may think it goes far enough. It may fully meet the views that is to say, of the party of Devolution. The Nationalists are likely to accept it as an improve- Inent of Irish administration. The Orangemen "ill oppose it, because "it is their nature to." The in itself is not open to serious objections from "Y side. Everybody will now perceive how Premature Mr. BALFOUR. was when, as a prelimin- arY to the General Election, he began a violent Performance on the anti-Home Rule drum. The 'Bill cannot even be regarded as a step towards Tiome Rule in the generally accepted meaning of that term. As Mr. BIRRELL himself said, if the zneasure is a success, it may help Home Rule, and if it is a failure it may impede it. This means, of Course, that it gives to Irishmen some further con- trol over their affairs, and that the question of 'ivhether they shall ultimately obtain full control Will depend on the use which they make of the Opportunity that is now to be given to them. Mr. BIRRELL'S Bill deals with purely'administra- tive reforms. It does not contain, as Mr. BIRRELL said, a touch or trace, a hint or suggestion, of any new legislative power or authority. It does not make a holocaust of Dublin Castle, even. No new laws, either public or private, can be made under its provisions. It gives no power to inter- ere, either with taxation or with rates. It affects the Parliamentary representation of Ireland in no WaY whatever. It is a Bill for the reorganisation of certain public departments, for placing them Under a limited public control, for oiling the

Wheels of the machinery of Irish Government, as it were, for preventing waste of money and of effort, for reducing the influence of officialism, and fur introducing a more generous and useful system Of expenditure in connection with departmental 'Work in Ireland. To some extent it will remove

the Irish grievance concerning over-taxation, Thel (lePartments which are to come undar the newly- Constituted body will have the power of spending nearly three-quarters of a million annually more than is expended at present. BIRRELL defined the object of- his measure :ith his customary grace and lucidity. This is the association of the sentiments of the Irish People as a whole with the administration of the numerous statues, rules, and regulations which direct the 'conducts of 'purely Irish affairs within Ireland herself." By what means? The means are simplicity itself. There is to be a Council in consisting of 107 members. Eighty-two Of these are to be elected, and twenty-four to be ' nominated, in the first place by the Crown, and

subsequently by the VICEROY. The remaining member will be the Under-Secretary for Ireland, who is at present SIR ANTHONY MACDONNELL. The eighty-two representative

members are to be elected under the franchise under which County Councils and other public bodies are ejected. The new Council is to have control of the work of the Irish Local Government Board, of the Agricultural and Technical Instruction Depart- ment, of the Congested Districts Board, and so on. It will have nothing to do with the courts of law, the police, the Land Commission, or the Prisons Board. There will be a new Education Depart- ment, under the control of the new Council, which will have the power of appointing committees. A great feature of the Bill is the removal of the barrier which stands in the way of the appoint- ment of a Roman Catholic as Lord Lieutenant, which means the disappearance of the last legal disqualification on religious grounds. The Bill might fairly be described as non-controversial. Mr. BALFOUR, nevertheless, soon made it appear that it is to be the subject of bitter controversy.

Warrington has a reputa- tion for patriotism to main- tain, 9, n d Empire Day affords a fitting and annual opportunity. The celebra tion of the late Queen's birthday by a recognition of imperial responsibilities bids fair to become an institution. The idea has much to recommend it, but if the day were devoted entirely to national self-glorification we should see in it more to criti- cise than to commend. We hear a lot about the Empire in these days, and the word has a pleasant sound in the ears of the proud Briton, but we would do well to occasionally recollect that even empires have their day and cease to be. Fortun- ately, the practical side of the celebrations is not being lost sight of. Opportunity is to be taken of impressing on the rising generation the duties of citizenship, which is more proper than imbuing them with the idea that the prerogatives of Empire are a God-given heritage. Apparently the adult citizens of Empire in Warrington are to be content with a display of Union Jacks and the hoarse screaming of works hooters. All we can deduce from the suggestions of the Empire Day Committee on their behalf 13 that they are to recollect that "the strongest ties to bind the Empire together are likely to be those of a com- mercial nature." This is putting sentiment in the pocket with a vengeance—but we were ever a nation of shop-keepers. Other arrangements for the day include special historical lessons in the schools, addresses on patriotism and citizenship etc., embellished by the exhibition of the Mayor's portrait in the Secondary school. Tfie " young idea" ought to be appreciably impressed and benefited. The clergy and ministers are invited to tam the occasion to good account in the pulpit this Sunday.

EMPIRE DAY.

Warrington has lost another THE LATE MR. old and highly respected PETER CALDWELL. townsman in the person of Mr. Peter Caldwell who at one time played a very active part in the com- mercial life of the town and district. Few men were more highly esteemed, for he possessed in a marked degree the gifts of kindliness and gener- osity, while his cheery disposition made everyone soon feel at home in his company. The old folk's treat was an institution particularly dear to his heart, and he took a real pleasure in seeing the aged guests thoroughly enjoy themselves. Mr. Caldwell may be said to have materially helped to promote the prosperity of Stockton Heath for he was the head of one of its old-established and flourishing industries. The hobby in which he chiefly delighted was music and he threw his whole heart into the work of the organization formed for the encourament of the divine art. Despite a physical handicap, he was no mean performer on the violin, and he had rendered enthusiastic service as a member of leading local orchestras. He was also a vocalist and loved to recall the days when he figured with many local worthies in the old Phil- harmonic Society and the Musical Society. Warrington will lament the loss of one who always took the liveliest interest in the town's affairs, while Stockton Heath in particular will miss the familiar figure of the benevolent-looking old gentleman who, to the last, found pleasure in the beauties of the countryside.

The American Presidency is MR. ROOSEVELT'S a long-contested office. The SUCCESSOR. business of fighting for the next possession of White House begins as soon as the last contest has con- cluded, although half a decade must elapse before the election can actually take place. Mr. Roosevelt, directly after his election, declared that he would not stand again; and, although he might be induced to depart from that decision, there are signs that no great pressure may be put upon him. The President has suffered some checks lately. The Harriman controversy, in spite of his denial of knowledge of the transaction which has been exposed, has obviously weakened his hold on the public somewhat, and there is a recrudes- cence of the feeling that a reversion to the tradi- tional policy of the United States with regard to outside affairs wpuld be wise. Possibly The Hague Conference may do something to restore Mr. Roosevelt's position in this respect. But, even so, the desire for a change may be too strong. Mr, Bryan apparently is conviced that he will be chosen to carry the Democratic banner in the fight. He is still the strongest man in his party. Judge Parker was a stop-gap; Mr. Hearst has gone under. Mr. Bryan has a discouraging record behind him, but he is a great fighter, and his ideas have been broadened and brought more closely into sympathy with the aspirations of his countrymen by. his tour round the world and his contact with peoples and policies outside the American continent. His opponent, if Mr. Roosevelt does not stand, is not unlikely to be Mr. Charles E. Hughes, who defeated Mr. Hearst in the fight for the Governor- ship of New York. Mr. Hughes has already won commendation by his courageous administration of New York State. Probably, however, his future will depend upon the results of his efforts against the corporations. He has a stiff fight, but if he wins Mr. Bryan may well tremble or his chance of ever occupying White House.

Latchford Wesley Guild. At the meeting on Thursday the Rev. John Gunnell gave an enjoyable paper, entitled "The spice of life and letters." Mr. Gunnell gave selections from the works of several famous writers and said the mental not less than the physical calls for pleasant seasoning and whilst all honour mast be given to the writers of our historical and philosophical books, our indebted- ness to Lamb, Dickens, Wendell Holmes and others must not be lost sight of. Mr. Ditchfield presided over the meeting.
