NOTES AND NOTIONS.

The Spanish heir has arrived, and England rejoices with Spain in the happy event. Gentlemen, your glasses !—Alfonso, Ena, and the infant prince ! ! * *

The dream of an illuminated tram car came true on Tuesday evening. Hundreds of townspeople who were not football enthusiasts took a delight in the glittering spectacle which was a credit to the Club and to the Tramvvay Department. * * * Shakespearean students and young tragedians will learn with the deepest regret that in consequence of the protest of the Danish Ambassador there will be no further performances of " Hamlet." It is understood that Archdeacon Colley has been offered the ghost for his private collection.—" Referee." 4, * The erection of the Infirmary extension is proceed- ing with wonderful rapidity and the townspeople can now obtain some idea of the imposing dimensions of the building which will fill such a long-felt want. There is every indication that the bazaar which will be held at Hill Cliffe in June in aid of the scheme will be a grand success.

* ,k The Strand Guardians have had to appoint a thin assistant to the boiler cleaner, who has become so stout that on the last occasion when he got inside the boiler great difficulty was experienced in extricating him. Possibly he remarked when imprisoned: "Oh, that this too solid flesh would melt !" If he did, the boiler fire should have been lit at once.

* * What was termed the "glorious resurrection " of an organ drew a large congregation to Emmanuel Church on Wednesday. It was fitting that the instru- ment which has for fifty years done duty in Bold-street should find its home in another church in that thoroughfare—in fact, one speaker said it would it would have been almost a tragd.y to have taken it any- where else. Fifty years is a long spell in the life of an organ, but this instrument appears to have been-quite rejuvenated, full at Wednesday's recital delighted the congregation with its sweet and powerful tones. The members of Emmanuel Church are to he congratulated upon their enterprise and under the direction of their hard-working pastor they should continue to go a-head.

* * The Stockton Heath Parochial Committee, on Tues- day, was proceeding to appoint sub-committees, and the names of the gentlemen vi ho had served during the past year had been read oat when Mr. Bouchier rose and said he wished to enter a protest. During the year he had been summoned to attend every meeting of the Fire Appliances Committee whereas he now discovered that he was not a member of that Committee. The Committee were not sympathetic. Instead they treated the matter as a huge joke and made amends by adding Mr. Bouchier's name to the list of gentlemen whose du,ty it is to see to the efficiency of the fire appliances, despite that gentle- man's protest that he had no wish to serve on the sub-committee.

Physical fitness went a long way towards winning Warrington the Northern Union Cup, but there will not be much of that finess left shortly judging, by the way the team is being feted. There was a dinner on Saturday, another on Monday, and there are two more big events of a similar kind yet to come off. Next Friday, Sir Gilbert Greenall will entertain the men at Walton Hall, and on Saturday they will be the guests of Mr. A. H.Crosfield,M.P.,at Hoylake. It was a great disappointment to the Borough Member that sudden illness prevented him witnessing the final, for he had the pleasure of seeing the Cup won by the Warrington lads at Leeds in 1905. On that occasion, so rumour says, Mr. Crosfield lost his hatband in an outburst of enthusiasm.

THE WHITSUNTIDE HOLIDAYS.

Where to Go.

With the approach of the Whitsuntide holidays many people will be contemplating a search for" fresh fields and pastures new." Should the weather be propitious there is sure to be a great exodus to popular seaside and country resorts,exceptionally good arrangements having been made by the railway and steamship companies.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0055.txt | included=true | included =====
GREAT CENTRAL RAILWAY.

The Great Central Railway Company advertise a splendid programme of express trains at excursion fares to be run from Warrington and district to London, Oxford, Stratford-on-Avon and Aylesbury, Bournemouth, Isle of Wight, Torquay, and the Sunny South, Folke- stone and Dover for Paris &c., Bath, Bristol, Cardiff and the West Coast, The Dukeries, Cleethorpes, and the East Coast. A non-stop during car express to Exeter, Plymouth and the Cornish Riviera is announced, and special attention is drawn to the Continental trips from Grimsby.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0056.txt | included=true | included =====
GREAT WESTERN RAILWAY.

The Great Western Railway Company is again offering full programme of excursions to cover the Whitson Holidays. The programme embraces holiday resorts in Somerset, Devon, Cornwall, Weymouth,Channel Islands, North and South Wales, Ireland, the Nlidlands, &e., &c., and the large number of places to which excursions will be run make it certain that every individual taste is catered for. The train services of the G.W.R. are deservedly famous, the smooth-running track being described as the best in the Kingdom. The fares are low, the services quick, and everything done to ensure the com- fort of the passengers. At all the holidays resorts there is good accommoda- tion for all classes, hotel, boarding house, appartments &c. Enquiries should be made at the nearest G.W.R. station or (ace for full details of train services, excur- sions, week-end and tourist bookings, &e. LONDON AND NORTH-WESTERN RAILWAY ARRANGENIENTS.

A large and varied list of cheap trips is scheduled to I be run by the London and North-Western Railway Com- pany from stations in this district. As is their usual custom, the trips by this company should meet all classes desiring to spend the holidays with friends, or at the seaside, and to some of the most important we would draw attention. On Saturday May 18th, cheap trips will be run te York, Durham, Newcastle and the North-eastern District? to Scotland by the West Coast Royal Mail Route on May 17, 22 and 23rd. On May 17, 18, 20 and each day in Whitweek to Dublin, Cork, Killarney and other interior stations in Ireland, by the short sea. route via. liolyhead. On May 18 and 20th for Cheltenham and Gloucester, and 17th I and 20th Bristol and Bath and on May 17th by the popular route via the Severn Tunnel to the %Vest of ' England. Special trains will be run to London (Euston) on May 17th and 18th and cheap excursion tickets will also be issued on May 20, 24, and 25th. To Chester and North Wales on May 17, 18 and 20 and a special train to Frodsham and Chester on the 20th.On May 17th,and 18th, to South and Central Wales, 17,18, and 20th to South Coast watering places, also to the Eastern Counties on the 18th. _ _

Numerous other excursions have been arranged to Birmingham, Blackpool, Huddersfield, Leeds and Man- chester. In addition to the special facilities which are announced, the week-end tickets issued on Friday and Saturday will be available to return on Sunday (where train service permits) Monday, Tuesday or Wednesday.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
NORTH WALES SAJLINGS.

The Liverpool and North Wales Steamship Company announce special arrangements for the Whitsuntide holidays. The usual daily sailings at 10.45 to Llandudno Beaumaris, and Bangor, have commenced, and on and after Friday, May 17th there will be an extended service to Menai Bridge. Isle of Anglesey trips have been arranged for Whit Sunday, 9.15 a.m., Whit Monday, 10 a.m., and Whit Tuesday 9.15 a.m. The Whit Monday sailings are as follows :-9.30 a.m., Llandudno and Carnarvon ; 10 a.m.," St. Tudno," Anglesey; and 10.45, "La Marguerite "to Llandudno, Beautnaris and Bangor.

LICENSING COMPENSATION.

Apportioning Warrington Awards.

A special meeting of the Warrington Licensing Com- pensation Authority will be held at the Police Court on Thursday morning for apportioning the compensation i awards, in respect of the three confiscated licenses, amongst the persons interested in the businesses. The owners of the houses are : Rope and Anchor Inn (£1330), Messrs. Greenall Whitley and Co.; Queen's Hotel (£1,570), Corporation of Warrington Boars Head Inn (21,620), Mrs. Bessie Fraser (Messrs. Peter Walker and Son lessees) ' • and the magistrates will be called upon to divide the compensation awards, given in narenthesis, between them and the respective tenants who are, Mr. William Donworth, Mr. RiChard Sanson, ' and the executors of the late Mr. Alfred Wood.

The Examiner, Saturday, May 11,1907 FATALITY AT THE ROYAL COURT] NOTES ON GAMES AND THEATRE. ATHLETICS.

Crane Collapses.

A shocking accident, attended with fatal results, occurred on Tuesday at the Royal Court Theatre, which is being re-built. Owing to the collapse of a derrick crane some masonry was dislodged and fell upon John Hinds, labourer, 17, Fennel-street, injuring him so terribly that he died in the Infirmary the same night. The circumstances of the accident were detailed at the inquest, which was held at the Police Station at noon yesterday by Mr. S. Brighouse, County Coroner. Mr. Sumner, Inspector of Factories, was present on behalf of the Home Office ; Mr. C. H. Unsworth represented Messrs. Hughes and Stirling, contractors, who are re-building the theatre, and Messrs. J. B. Johnson and Co., by whom the deceased was employed; and Mr. T S. Steel appeared for the relatives of the deceased. The Coroner pointed out that deceased was employed by Messrs. J. B. Johnson and Co., slaters and plasterers, Liverpool and Warrington' but they did not enter into the story at all, as they had nothing to do with what happened. Messrs. Hughes and Sterling, the building contractors, had to more a crane which was on the top of the Royal Court Theatre. The process of removal was in progress when the crane over-balanced, collapsed and came down, striking some masonry which became detached, and part of which fell upon deceased, inflicting fatal wounds. Robert Kirk 156, Evelyn-street, Sankey Bridges, who had been employed by Messrs. Hughes and Stirling about eight months, said the crane was fixed by the foreman labourar and six other men about nine days ago. On Tuesday the crane was being dismantled by witness and another man named Green, under instruc- tions they had received from the foreman. Tlapir orders were to take part of the weight off the back stays and then to go to the foreman for assistance to take the crane down. They had lightened the stays and the foreman was coming on the scene when the crane quite unexpectedly fell over. He could not account for the collapse, but it could not have , happened if they had not removed some of the weight from the stays. The Coroner: If anything it is an error of judg- ment in perhaps taking more away than you ought to have done. I don't say that you are to blame for it. No one is perfect in this world. Most people think

_ they are, but I never found one. In answer to Hr. Steel witness said no warning was was given to the men working below when the crane

was i-o be moved.

The Coroner said it did not require much intelligence to grasp the fact that when the foundations were taken away the crane must be more insecure than it was before. The object was to make it insecure so as to take it down. No doubt the men • removed what they

thought was a reasonable amount. No one would sug- gest that they took any more than thoy ought to do so as to injure one of their fellow creatures. Questioned by Mr. Unsworth, witness said they had taken down the crane once before from another part of the building and no accident °coursed. A Juror suggested that the jib of the crane might have been taken off before the foundations were loosened, but witness pointed out that the jib was used to lower the stones from the foundations,

Frederick Burkin, foreman labourer,said he instructed the men to lighten the weight on the crane. His opinion was that the jib had been lowered and drawn the centre post from its socket. Probably too much weight had been taken from the back stays and consequently the crane collapsed. The Coroner emphasised the foolishness of men making suggestions as to how a business should be con- ducted when they themselves had not much knowledge of it. If the workmen tried to move the crane in a business-like way, the fatality must be a pure accident. The longer he lived the more adverse he was to blaming a firm or individual § unless he was honestly certain that he was right and they were wrong. The jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death." Mr. Unsworth, on behalf of Messrs. Hughes and Stirling, Messrs. J. B. Johnson and Co., the proprietors of the theatre, and the architect, expressed deep regret at the accident, and sympathy with the widow and family. He had not the slightest doubt that those feel- ings would be expressed in tangible form. Mr. Steel said the deceased had left six children, only one of whom was married. It was pleasing to know that in all probability there would be no litigation with regard to compensation.

Departure of the Rev. C. L. Stowe.

Primitive Methodists of Warrington will learn with regret of the impending departure of the Rev. C. L. Stowe, who will sever his connection with Warrington on Juno 23rd, when he will join the more important circuit of Eccles. Mr. Stowe come to this town three years ago from Wishaw, Scotland, where he served his four years probation after leaving college, and through- out has held a high place in the affection and esteem of those amongst whom he ministered. The most marked feature of his labour in Warrington has been that he has made the circuit entirely self-supporting. He will be succeeded by the Rev. John B. Buglas.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
Bewsey-road Wesleyan Church. Sunday School Anniversary.

Special services were held at Beivsey-road Wesleyan Church on Sunday in celebration of of the anniv3rsary of Silver-street Sunday school. There were very large congregations, friends from all parts of the circuit attending. The preacher was the Rev. J. Grange Bennett, a member of one of the best-known and most respected Methodist families in the district, and his eloquent discourses were highly appreciated. The musical portion of the services was most enjoyable and special praise is due to Mr. W. Hipkiss who must have devoted considerable time to training the children. In the morning the choir gave the anthem" 0 worship the Lord" (Hollins), the tenor solo being effectively interpreted by Mr. J. E. Hannan. In the afternoon there was a special choral service , when a most interesting programme was submitted, " List to the Cherubic Host" (Gaul) was very credit- ably rendered, the quartette being taken by the Misses Hipkiss, Leicester, Evans and Fletcher and the baritone solo by Mr. E. H. Pearce. Mrs. Hannan sang "The last milestone " with great expression and the choir rendered" Glorious is Thy Name" to the music of the Gloria in Mozart's 12th Mass. The service was pre- sided over by Mr. John Upson,who eulogised the work of the school, which, he said, had had a career of unparalleled prosperity. Ho pointed out that large uumbers of young people had become attached to the church. They were determined they would not stand still in the Sunday School. They had solved to a large extent the problem of attaching their young people to the church. The adult classes were the largest in the school, and they had scholars, men and women, who had reached the age of 60 years. Dr. Ferguson gave art address on "He was numbered among the transgressors" and combatted some of the teaching put forth by advocates of the New Theology. The Church was filled to its utmost capacity in the evening, when the Rev. J. Grange Bennett took for his text the words : "I beseech you, therefore, brethren, by the mercies of God, that ye present your bodies a living sacrifice, holy, acceptable unto God, which is your reasonable service." The preachPr contradicted the statement which had been put forward that the great aim of the Christian Church was to save souls, but that the Christian religion neglected the body. Take, for instance, the Salvation Army and the Church Army, and their own church, which catered for the social and material benefit of men and women, as well as for their spirttual benefits. Take their own church at Manchester. His friend, Mr. Collier, was doing a magnificent work. Primarily his object was to save the souls of the people but he was, thankful to say that besides that he was caring for the bodies and catering for their material well-being. He appealed to the young men in the congre- gation to present their bodies as a living sacrifice to God, and if they did so every member would be engaged in holy work. The choir gave the recit, chorus, trio and grand chorus from Haydn's " Creation,' the tenor solo being sung by Mr- W. Tynan and a trio by Miss Hipkiss, Messrs. Sinclair and Jane. The children sang the hymn "When morning comes in glory" exceptionally well. It should be stated that the choir was quite unaugmented so that the music reflected particular credit upon the members and especially upon Mr. Hi pk iss who conducted. Mr. T. W. Pearce ably presided at, the organ. The collections for the day realized over £3O.

Cricket.

At last the weather has been kind and on Saturday the Warrington eleven took the field for the first time this season. According to the fixture card the match was the third on the list, but it was the first to be played, as the other two had to be cancelled owing to the rain. Saturday's encounter with Prescot was the opening league engagement, and Warrington had n difficulty in registering a substantial victory. The Prescot batsmen, with the exception of Sharp, the pro- fessional who made 33, were in wretched form and their innings closed for 59. Cresswell and 11.Callon wrought havoc with the ball, each accounting for five wickets at a cost of 19 and 35 runs respectively.

Warrington entered into their task with spirit and compiled 192, so that they have made a capital start with League cricket. The most notable feature of their innings was the splendid display by Cresswell, the pro- fessional, who ran up 63 by free and remarkably clean hitting. A. Duckworth helped materially to bring about the victory, his innings realizing 44, which included seven boundaries. J. W. Bibby, the Warrington captain, was also in fine form, and made 36 in faultless style, hitting five fours. Warrington are determined to make a bold bid for the cup this season, and if Saturday's match is to be taken as a criterion, there should be little fear as to the success of their efforts.

The second eleven cut a sorry figure at Prescot, where a very weak team did duty for Warrington. The visitors only made 57 as against Prescot's 106, the only Warrington player to attain double figures being A. Thewlis (16). The eleven has a good bowler in the person of T. Waywell who captured seven wickets for 29 runs, while Thewlis accounted for three at a cost of 43. We ara expecting better things from Warrington 2nd as the season progresses and we hope a bad start will only have the effect of spurring the players on to more creditable deeds.

Local enthusiasts will ly) pleand to hear that the Works Competition which was such a great success last season is to be repeated. The opening match will be played on Tuesday, .May 14th.

Sankey Wire Mills visited salubrious Widnes on Saturday hut failed to pull off a victory in their match with White Rose. They were beaten by 56 runs to 40, their only batsman to make double figures being J. Elawthorn (20). Widnes also had only one player who attained that distinction—Hewitt, whose total was 26.

Penketh entertained Runcorn and defeated them by the narrow margin of 5. J. Dunhill anci H. Davies (14 each) were the top scorers for the villagers.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
Bowling.

A handbook which should prove of considerable value to lovers of this sport is the bowling annual which has just been issued by Niessrs. E. Hulton and Co., Ltd., at the price of 3d. This is the first year of publication, and the book contains records and rules of amateur and professional bowling. Last year's results, and fixtures for the present season are given of the Lancashire, Yorkshir6-,Cheshire, and Midland Counties Associations. A feature is the complete return of the great Blackpool Sweepstakes. The dimensions of the chief greens will also doubtless be of interest.

The bowling season opened in earnest on Saturday and for the next few months trundlers will indulge to their hearts' content in the summer sport. Locally interest centres chiefly in the doings of the works teams in the annual struggle for the championship, and if the opening day's games can be taken as a sample of what is to follow the play will be followed more closely than ever. The bowling green at Bank Quay, the scene of the operations, is in excellent condition and a large crowd was present to witness the bowling of the first wood. Fletchers No. 3 and Wire Ropery had the honour of opening the play and treated the spectators to an excellent and exciting exhibition which ended in the VViremen claiming the right to enter the second round by a margin of one point only.

To come to the play, Ropery at the end of the first game claimed a lead of ten points but an immediate reduction by C. Ashton was an earnest of the neck and neck race which was to follow. With three points separating the teams J. Looker ran away from his opponent and despite a rally claimed the verdict by five points and placed his side eight points to the good. The advantage gained was, however, immediately lost as after a long fight Houghton placed his team again within three points of the leaders. Fletcher's strove hard to get the upper hand and Randles achieved the object by trouncing his opponents 15-3 and with the match half over the scores stood Fletcher's 73, Ropery 6G. Perry Came to the rescue of his side and winning by 15-9 carried the score to 82-81. Ropery dropped another point but they were not fated to remain long in arrears and a victory by Clive gave them the lead by 4 chalks.

Matters, to say the least of it, were in a highly interesting stage and the excitement was increased as Regan placed the team level by winning 15-11. Allen came a cropper and Fletcher's with eleven men off the green led by three points. It was seen that the last couple were having a close fight, and when Henderson went to the front 10-8 Fletchers indulged in visions of victory. These were heightened when their man secured the next end. Already they were beginning to congratulate themselves and their dismay could be imagined when Lingard found his length and in two ends drew level, and then forged ahead and with a double won his side the match. Fletcher's were this beaten on the post and their only consoling thought was that they had put up a good fight.

The other match proved a runaway affiir as Firth Company experienced no difficulty in disposing of Stockton Heath Forge whose score they more than doubled.

To-day's matches are Whitecross (No. 2) v. C.L.C., Fletohers (No. 2) v. Lockers and Penketh Tannery v. Rylands.

DEATH -OF MR. PEfER CALDWELL.

A prominent Warrington commercial gentleman passed away at his residence, Walton-road, Stockton Heath, on Saturday, in the person of Mr. Peter Caldwell, founder of the firm of Messrs. Caldwell, Ltd., Elorsemarket- street. Born in Warrington 75 years ago Mr. Caldwell belonged to an old Warrington family, his father being one of the firm of Caldwell, Parker and Co., Scotland- road, Warrington, ironfounders and engineers. He served his apprenticeship with the engineering firm of Richard Kitchin, Scotland-bank, Warrington. He met with a serious accident whilst erecting machinery for them at Tunbridge Wells, and had to submit to the amputation of his arm, and thus incapacitated from the manual part of his trade he was for some years in the employ as representative and traveller of Messrs. N Greening and Sons, Ltd. Then he commenced the busi- ness of Millfurnisher and Engineer's Stores at Winwick- street, removing to Market-street, and finally building the present premises in florsemarket-street and Scot- land-road, Warrington. He was instrumental in bringing the indiarubber trade to Warrington, being the executor of tile late Mr. John Taylor, the proprietor at that time of the Castle Rubber Works. About the year 1885 he purchased the old-established Spade and Shovel Manu- factory at Stockton Heath, which has been in existence since 1770, and had been successively in the hands of the Hamblett family, and of the late Mr. John Alker. At one time be was a member of the Borough Council and of the Board of Guardians, but had not for some years taken an active interest in local matters. He was a member of the local committee for providing the aged poor with the annual treat, and though of late years infirmities prevented him being present at the STANLEY LORD. meetings, he took a keen interest in the proceedings to THE VALUATION BILL. the last. THE VOICE OF LABOUR.

Edited by John Workman.

Readers of this column are invited to assist in making it interesting and helpful to social workers by contributing themselves. Correspondents must write on one side of the paper only, and endeavour to make their letters and notes short and to the point. A nont-de-plume may be used, but the real name of the writer should be forwarded in confidence to the Editor of the column. M.S.S, must be addressed to John Workman, " Examiner " Office Warrington and should be sent in not later than Wednesday in each week.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0068.txt | included=true | included =====
MOVE Hllf UP,

A man who is placed in authority should not seek to draw all to himself by feeling that he is a centre to which all remuneration should come,' but if he is a true benefactor to others he will seek to give credit to each man for the good that is in him and will treat that man as he would like to be treated himself. Why should he not acknowledge the capabilities of others and be willing to help his fellow-workmen to live up to their standard of usefulness?

_ Why should a steady young man be kept at one post year after year when he is capable of being moved up? It is discouraging to a man, when he sees a vacancy which would be an advantage to him to fill, to be kept tea solely because it would give his foreman a little extra trouble to train another man into doing his work. It is little comfort to be told that he cannot do without him.

Foremen should not think of themselves at these times, but rather of the advantage of others, for in so doing they bring peace and happiness to themselves, gain the respect of the workmen and also create in them a truer feeling to work for the benefit of their masters.

BE MEN.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
WEALTH DISTRIBUTION.

He who seeks to voice the aspirations or the claims or the needs of the worker in these highly complex , days of national finance and income, needs at times to struggle with the Income Tax returns and select some reminders of the huge' discrepancy between toil and the reward therefor. Our means of wealth-making have for the time outstripped our methods of wealth distribution, and the . problem of the present century is chiefly —wealth distribu- tion and all that it entails. 16.000 persons have an aggregate income of £183,000,000, or roughly speaking £ll,OOO each per annum. 259,000 persons have incomes of between £7OO and £5,000 per year, but 38,000,000 have incomes (I mean pittances) of £25 per year. In 1881 the gross profits of public companies, including railways, mines, were £76,000,000. In 1905 they were £247,000,000,an increase of 200 per cent., and we must not forget that these increases represent added burdens on the backs of the workers, as dividends and interests in public companies are the reward not of toil or skill or management, but are a tax on, industry imposed by the owners of the means of wealth production. After all, even a millionaire can only sleep in one bed at a , time or reside in one house at a time, and it is simply ' a physical impossiblity for great riches to be used by the reputed owner. The balance inevitably goes to all sorts- and conditions of hangers on, good, bad and indifferent, and the far flashing brilliance of modern civiliatien is the brilliance of a bonfire, destroying in order to shine, rather than the light of a radiant sun invigorating all. In attempting to readjust the inadequate distribution we remember that the State has no right to tax individuals unless it is prepared and can put the money to a more useful purpose. The needs of the community must be the measure of taxation. Social reform must not remain unachieved while untaxed unearned increment abounds. If the worker of all grades and kinds cannot obtain his fair .share at the source in immediate payment for his toil, then the community must get it back for national needs of social justice by means of taxation of those who reap where they have not sown. I pray daily for a brave Chancellor of the Exchequer.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0070.txt | included=true | included =====
LA.ZARUS,

ViTHO'S TO BLAME? Jack Jones, which is not his real name, is just sixty years of age; he is still active and energetic and though age has somewhat dimmed his eyes and time left its indelible impress upon his one time raven locks he is by no means worn out. Jack, I may mention, is neither a sinner nor a saint, he has in his time quaffed many flagons of "sixpenny," he has done many things which he ought not to have done 'and has, in all probability, left iladone those things which he ought to have done; in a phrase', Jack is an average man, like most of us a curious mixture of good and evil, the product of circumstances and ancestry. Jack as I have stated is sixty years of age, he has been married twice and his second wife and a youngish family are dependent upon him for their daily bread. Now Jack is employed by a limited company who have to pay a dividend of six .per cent on their preference ,shares and who find it increasingly difficult to do so. Last week they decided' to part with their old servants and to engage in their stead younger, quicker and cheaper men, consequently Jack will shortly be out of work and unless he is lucky will sodn have to appeal to the Workhouse His wife may earn little money by charing but it is almost a certainty that his family will in future know the want of good and sufficient food and clothing. Jack is only one of many who are similarly situated, when a man wants in efficiency modern industry discards him. A machine may be worked when it is obsolete because it costs money to

replace it with anew one, but there are always new men ready to take the places of the older ones who are discarded. Now who is to blame? Jack who has work hard

the best part of his life for the profit of others cannot be held responsible. The Manager of the Firm cannot help the circumstanees because he must either make the business pay or " get out." The directors mast compete with other firms ih the same trade otherwise they would not get sufficient trade to pay the six per cent. dividend. The share- holders have always been taught to expect interest on 'capital and in. many cases such interest is their sole support. Thus nobody directly concerned seems to be responsible for the fact that Jack Jones will be "out of work" and his children will want bread. Is not the plight of Jack Jones a fate to which many of us are hastening, sufficient to convi.nce anyone that our system of s)ciety is wrong? , So long as competition is the foundation Of industry, so long as the private monopoly of land and capital obtains', so long will many thousands be in the same case as Jack Jones. Jack ! if you and your fellows had made up your minds to change the structure of society, to substitute public ownership of land and capital Nfor private ownership, and to enthrone co-operation in place of competition you would not have now been creeping under he dark shadow of the poor house. But that of course would have been Socialism, and Socialism as all the hard-headed know is a dream. But starvation is real, hunger is real, unemployment is no vision, .and the ten-a-penny 1 politicians have no remedy.

"Methods of Barbarism."

Sir,—What is the matter with your contemporary, the "Observer?' It appears that my letter on the Grappen- hall meeting of the Primrose League has caused some uneasiness. The writer of the" Observer" notes says that Mr. Howes suggested that Mr. Charlton Parr was • the inventor of the phrase" methods of barbarism," as applied to the late War. Of course, I did nothing of the sort. I distinctly stated that Mr. Parr accused Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman, of applying those remarks to the British soldier. Thus Mr. Parr was repeating a stale and oft refuted slander, but our friend of the "Observer "has looked up the quotation from Sir Henry's speech, which proves my contention that Sir Henry was speaking of the general conduct of the War. Of coarse, our Tory friends know full well that we blamed the Tory Government for their terrible mis- management of the South African business, whereby they caused the strife to be prolonged, our national resources to be wasted, and the life's blood of our brave soldiers to be shed to a very unnecessary extent. Men, lam accused of alleging that Mr. Parr said that the late Sir Wilfrid Lawson is the Attorney- General, and lam told that I ought to have said that the present Attorney-General is Sir John Lawson Walton. Now, Sir, when I read this latest bit of Tory criticism, I was sorely perplexed. I eagerly read over again my letter, published in the "Examiner "of April 27th, and I of course discovered that I never referred to Sir Wilfrid Lawson or the Attorney-General in any sense whatever It is evident that something has gone wrong with the works at the" Observer" Office. The writer says: "Mr. Howes can occasionally be accurate in his statements." I have no doubt that lean truthfully return the compliment to our friends of the "Observer," only after reading their recent notes, I must, in their case, emphasise the word occasionally— Yours etc., 3. HO WEB,

Morecambe, May 9th.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
The Title Reverend.

Sir,—C. F. Aked, a Baptist minister of Liverpool, sailed for New YOrk on the 9th of April, from which city he had "a call" some time before. As these calls are always made and accepted on a rising scale we may be sure that the change has been for the pastor's wordly advantage. It will be remembered that it was this man's public renunciation of the prefix reverend that produced some letters in" The Examiner" nine or ten months _ ago. As I can only trace once solitary follower of his example in this neighbourhood, may I ask you, Mr. Editor, to allow me to add one more contribution to my i share n that correspondence? And should you, with your usnsal liberality, concede the privilege, 1 trust my effort may be useful in opening the eyes of some unpre- judiced readers to the innovation upon Christianity involved in ministerial distinctions. Having, in my previous letters, gone fully into the precedents and precepts of Scripture which forbid such distinctions I will adopt a different treatment to-day. The Ruler of England is a very exalted personage and has in consequence many important titles conferred upon him. Now were the ministers of religion to apply one of these titles to themselves and strut before the public with it upon them the matter would soon reach the King's knowledge, and steps would be taken at once to stop arid punish the imposture. Fear may, therefore, have something to do in restraining the ministers from meddling with his Majesty's prerogatives. Or indeed it may be that an esrthly prefix would not satisfy their ambition. Be this as it may, it is certain that they do not hesitate to aporopriate an attribute of the King of Kings for their adornment. The God of Heaven has revealed that His Name is reverend, and these men, although professing to be His servants, ask us to believe that their names are reverend too! In all the wide range of human egotism there is no more daring or absurd claim than this, and the very men who make it know in their hearts that it is spurious, and unjustifiable upon any ground whatever. Creatures formed of clay And full of earthly passions and infirmities presuming to confer upon themselves a title of the Deity is a state of things so astoundine and so preposterous as to stagger hnnian belief. There is, however, no room alas,. for doubt 'about the matter, for the sectarian magazines,. the Press, arid the mural literature of our towns and cities all publish the enormous extent to which the claim,in its various degrees of comparison,is pushed. But. Heaven is much farther away than London, and is known . to defer judgment that the long-suffering of God may • lead sinners tp repentance and reform. And so the "clergy and ministers of religion" masquerade at will in their bprrowed plumes ; only a solitary voice, here. and there, being raised against them. I am_aware that the argument of "use and wont" is often brought forward in defence of institutions and practices that will not bear the light of truth. We are told that clerical titles are of very ancient date and. for this reason should be retained and respected. Indeed a long career is the only plea thet can be urged in their favour. They have been in use ever since the time of' the monk Augustine who .founded the English Church in Canterbury fourteen hundred years ago. But surely something more than longevity is required to justify a. usage like the one under discussion. Satan was deceiver at the beginning, and the various pulpits assure us that he is so still, no change having been wrought in. his character and disposition during the long probation of six thousand years. And a parity of reasoning forces the conclusion that a history of fourteen centuries cannot change the complexion of a custom that was and is contraband of Scripture and common sense. If my reader has any doubt upon this point a careful perusal of the first twelve verses of the twenty-third chapter of _ Matthew's gospel wlll dissipate it. Our friends of the Chinch and the Chapel are dis- pleased with any one who objects to the use of reverend by ministers of religion. And in attempting to argue their point they either are, or pretend to be, oblivious of the Lord's injunction against titles, and would have us believe that in calling a preacher reverend, very reverend or most reverend they are honouring Christ through his minister,! This, however, is a specimen of religious logic which I are not prepared to adopt, having no example in heavenly or earthly things for such a course. The angels worship Him that sitteth upon the throne direct, and not through any of , his subordinates; and one never sees or hears of a tutor in a nobleman's family putting on his master's title by way of honouring him in the eyes o f his household ! The natural and only way by which a servant can show his respect and devotion to his master is by willing and faithful compliance with his wishes known and under- stood. And the heavenly Master applies the same test to his followers when he says,—"lf ye love no, keep my commandments." (John xiv. 15). Now among all the instructions, verbal and by example, which Jesus bequeathed to his disciples for all time there is none more simple and emphatic than the one forbidding the use of titles by ministering servants. Therefore, when one sees our religious instructors acting as if this law had no existence, and assuming, not an earthly badge of distinction, but an attribute of Gad, it is surelx only legitimate criticism to say that they are too ambitious and may, possibly, have mistaken their calling alto- gether. Pins.lly, the assumption of one of the names of Glad by preachers is either right or wrong. If it is right there must be evidence to support it, but I have never seen any. If it is wrong, the sooaer the practice is abolished the better it will be for the spread of truth and true religious liberty. If, in writing on the subject as I have done, I have erred, either in matter or in judgment, I shall be grateful for correction,—Yours truly, April, 1907.

lONA.

The truth in a man iz hiz gratpst strength and hiz gratest glory; the angels in heaven fairly envy it. I have never allowed miaelf to kontrakt enny habits that I kan't disgorge, for I hay lernt that those old people who hay the fewest habits, or none at all, are the ones who are the most enjoyable. _ _

I hay some enernys in this world, and hay managed to vanquish all ov them bi never jaw- ing back. Az disgusting az snobbery iz, it iz playfull to kno.that every snob haz hiz toady.

The role oi•the monkey iz the mast unsatis- faktory one to attempt; if yu fill the role, yu hay only equaled a monkey; if yu git beat, where are yu? The man who reads every book he kan .find will git so phull of knolledge after awhile that he won't be certain of ennything. BALFOUR CAPTURED.

[By MR J. HowEs.]

The visit of the colonial Premiers will long be remembered in this country, and it is well that the Liberal Party has arranged that the Liberal confer- ences shall take place every four years, in order that we may have an opportunity of conferring with ,the representatives of the distant parts of the Empfre, from time to time. The Tories have done their best to make party capital out of this visit, although their party kept our colonies in bondage as long as possible, and by their short sighted policy lost us the grandest of our col.mial possessions (The American States). The Liberals, by granting self-government, have made these colonies, united and loyal; tberefore they have a right to claim to be the true Colonial Party. The" Birmingham Post" has recently snubbed the Tories, for trying to monopolise the position of ' Imperialists. "It is with a sense of shame," (says this Unionist paper)" that we hear those who are so ready to stoop to cheap party tactics, proclaim they are the only Imperialists --that they alone, stand for the great conceptions of Empire. It seems to behove responsible members of the Unionist party, to mark their disaproval of this shallow and vulgar dinning into the ears of the colonial premiers, that Codlin's your friend, not Short.' We are all friends of the Colonies, we all join in welcoming their distinguished representatives, and we all pray., that their meeting will be productive of good to the empire we love. Surely at such a time, it is only decent to hold reck- less party spleen in check." This vigorous rap on the knuckles will, perhaps do our Tory friends good. Sir Joseph Ward (New Zealand Premier) speaking .at lanchester, declared his" supreme satisfaction" with the work done at the conference, and said that every colonial representative had "received at the hands of the representatives of the British Government,the great- est courtesy, consideration and kindness in every instance." One of the benefits conferred upon us by the visit of our Colonial friends is seen in the latest utter- ance of ilfr Balfour. Ever since the last general election the Proteztionist section of the Tory party has been endeavouring to find out this gentleman's true opinions on their pet question. He, with all the skill of which he is master, has continued to juggle with the question, and by propounding a number of verbal riddles has succeeded in keeping up his reputation as the mystery man, and befogging his followers; hut at last he has declared himself, and thrown in his lot with the "whole hoggers." We Liberals, have no need to complain of his recent attitude. As long as the Tories stand wedded to protection the Liberal party is safe. Of course, Mr. Balfour may in his next speech explain his recent utterances away, but for the present we must consider him in his latest character. The Protectionists have captured him and they are naturally jubilant at the result, although one Tory paper asks, "does he mean it ?" and others seem to have some doubt upon the question. The revival of the old agitation is not to be known as Protection but Colonial Preference and we are still threatened that if we refuse to tax our people's food for the benefit of a portion of some of our Colonies, the Empire will be in danger, but this Tory nightmare has been killed by the Colonial premiers themselves. The New' Zealand Premier said, " I sincerely hope that no one in this country supposes that because we differ at the ' conference we are not going to stand loyal and true by the old land' because of these differences." , General Botha said that as the self-governing Colonies desired to be free to manage their own affairs, so it was urgent that the mother country should manage its own affairs. Though no preference were given, the bond between Great Britain ' and the Transvgal would not be strained. And other distinguished colonial representatives, have made similar declarations, therefore, whenever Tory speakers trot out this terrible bogie, we must remember that the Colonies repudiate the charge of probable disloyalty, , brought against them by the Tory party. Mr. Balfour tells us that two things have induced him to adopt his present position. He is fearful that the Liberal Party will not be able to carry out social reforms without a terrible increase of taxation This new-born zeal for social reforms, on the part of Mr. I Balfour, is really very touching, and his•horror of increased taxation reveals the gentleman in an entirely new character. I think we can show him a way out of his difficulties. If money is wanted for , old age Pensions, we can continue the process intro- duced by Mr. Asquith. Graduate the income tax, and increase the death duties, thus placing the charge of old age pensions for the workers upon the accumulated, abundant wealth of the enormously rich. Mr. Balfour says, "the Prime Ministers of the self governing Colonies, have come here with one. accord, to press the policy of preference upon us." Of course this is not true, for the Colonies are very much divided on the question. The" Toronto Globe" (Canada) says :—" There is but one way in which the people of Great Britian can favour the Colonies, and that is by paying them higher prices. . . . . If we get higher prices, through the Tariff or otherwise, the people of Great Britain must pay. "Whatever other Colonies may seek, Canada will neither seek nor support such a favour to the Colonies. The fact is, the people of Great Britain have other heavy public burdens. A large propor- tion of them are living close to the borders of want, and the struggle for existence is hard and continu- ous. Deeper reflection will show that such requests for artificially-enhanced prices, which the Colonies do not want, and which burden the Mother-country, are due not to selfishness, as it must appear to the British people, but to economic hallucinations obscuring the real nature of these requests. Canada does not claim to be more unselfish than other Colonies, but Canada's representation has sufficient economic insight to see the real purport of the demand for Protection in Great Britain.' CAPE TOWN, May 2nd. The "South African News" repudiates in emphatic terms the assertion reported to have been made by Dr. Jameson at the Imperial Conference to the effect that all politicians in Cape Colony were in favour of preference. During the last session of the Legislative Assembly the whole of the Bond Party, with the exception of two members, voted specifically against preference; and an anti-preference resolution was actually passed in the Legislative Council. The journal remarks that the Conference will be a fraud if countries comprised in thfra Empire are to be represented by.statesmen who misrepresent the Parliamentary minority, whilst themselves being in a minority among the electorate, as is notoriously the position of the Cape Premier.

Our ireat Indian possession is dead against any interference with our Free Trade policy. The repre- sentative of that part of our Empire said

"The interests of India di) not, therefore, call for any change. The purchasing power of India in British markets is largely dependent on her trade with foreign countries. This is shown by the export and import figures, which show that the British Empire sells fifty millions worth of goods to India and buys 39i millions sterling, whilst foreign countries sell to India eighteen millions sterling worth of goods and buy goods of the value of siztv-six millions sterling." •

What is meant by Colonial Preference? We are told that we must make a bargain with our Colonies, but a bargain should have two sides. Any one-sided deal would not be 's bargain but a monopoly, and we must be very careful that the Tories do not lead us into an arrangement of this sort. At this present time we give our Colonies open ports. We, allow them to send us, what they like, how they, like, when they like, and the cheaper they send their good things, the better we like them. Do they reciprocate our kindness? No. They ' erect a wall of Tariffs against our goods and now some of them ask us to put a tax on goods that come from other countries, in their supposed interests, and in return they promise not to take off the taxes they put on our goods and give us open ports, as we give them, but that they will tax foreign countries a little higher than they tax us, in other words, we are to cease getting things cheap from abroad, in order that we may get them dear from the Colonies. This is a very good arrangement for them, but a very bad one for us, and as Mr. Joseph Chamberlain has told us that if we give a preference to the Colonies, we must tax the people's food, I have no fear but that the British people will continue, in future as they have done in the past., to resist the re-enactment of this Tory crime.

LEEDS ROAD COLLAPSES.

A remarkable incident occurred in Clarendon-road, a residential thoroughfare in Leeds, shortly before noon on Wednesday. A motor-car had just passed when a large part of the centre of the road collapsed, exposing a cavity about 151 t. deep, and large enough to contain a horse and cart. It appears that an old well had been loosely filledfup. and when the filling subsided only a crust of macadam, barely 14in. thick, was left The judges who are sitting this week at the Leeds Assizes have been driven over the spot twice daily.

THE GOOD OLD TIMES.

'"Only nine bets were made in about an hour," said Mr. Enoch Evans, a Walsall solicitor in a betting case on Wednesday "whereas in the good old times there would have been 150 bets made in that time." A Magistrate: You mean the bad old times.

WARRINGTON

The Wesleyan Bazaar.

The splendid success of the Japanese bazaar in Parr Hall must be a source of gratification to the workers at Bold-street, for the proceedings were closed on Saturdqy when it was announced that the total proceeds amounted to the handsome sum of £1,500. Votes of thanks were accorded to all who had assisted to bring about such a gratifying result. The children's opening ceremony which took the form of the pretty cantata "Spring Triumphant" wa3 repeated and again afforded much pleasure. BABY CARRIAGES.—New and exclusive designs now showing.—J. S. OARLA.ND, 51, Bridge-street, Warrington.—Advt. Local Successes. ' At the recent examination of the Associated Board of the Royal Academy and the Royal College of Music, the following pupils of Miss Partington, Stockton Lodge High School, were successful in pianoforte playing: -Intermediate grade, local centre, the Misses Edith Partington and Josie Broome ; school examina- tions, lower division, Anse Marjorie Bennett ; primary division, Miss Gladys Hamman. At the annual eisteddfod promoted by the Tyldesley Welsh Baptists on Saturday, in the pianoforte solo (juniors), Charles Ewart Eckersley, Eadestown, was awarded the first prize, whilst Mr. E. H. Pearce, of Warrington, won th ; bass sole competition. Personal. We are pleased to announce that the Postmaster- General has been pleased to promote Mr. James W. Woods to the position of Assistant Engineer in Chief in the Post Office. Mr. Woods received his education at the Warrington People's College under Mr. Brewtnall, and commenced his business career in the United Kingdom Telegraph Company in Hersemarket- street. Mr. Woods is the son of the late Peter and Rachel Woods, of Winwick-street, and is brother to Mrs. W. Clegg, Irlam-street. SUITS made to measure.—BA.NKS, Outfitter.—Advt. Licensing Transfers.

At the Borough Police Court on Monday, Mr. F. G. Taylor (Messrs. Henry Greenall and Company) applied for the transfer of the licence of the British Tar, Winwick-road, to Arthur Moffat, step-son of the late landlady, Mrs. Ann Moffatt. No objection was offered, and the application was granted. The off-licence at 38, Finners-brow, was transferred from John Peake to Charles Hall, and temporary authority was granted Peter Lawless to sell at the Borough Arms in lieu of Enoch A. Hollingworth, ex-police sergeant. Whist Drive.

On Monday evening a successful whist drive pro- moted by the Young Ladies' club was held in the St. Clement's Schoolroom. The Rev. T. W. Barber and Mr. R. W. Collin acted as M.C.'s. The prizes which were given by Mr. H. Mather, were presented by Mrs. Barber to the following ladies and gentlemen :—Mrs. William Price (Ist. prize), Miss Pitman and Miss Horabin, and the Rev. J. H. G. Hollingworth and Messrs. Pierpoint and Jones. Mrs. Barber and Misses E. H. Boardman, Howard, Jones, Davies, and Edith Jones, of the Ladies' Club, had made all the arrange- ments. Dancing was afterwards indulged in till the early hours of Tuesday morning.

Considerable interest is being taken in the seventh annual horse parade which will take place on Saturday, May 25th. In the annual report just issued it is stated that the number of entries last year was 113, and that prizes amounting to £4l 2s. 6d. were distributed amongst the competitors. In addition to this two silver cups were given by Sir Gilbert Greenall, the president of the society ; a silver cup by Mr. Peter Davies, of Warburton; medals and premium by the Shire Horse Society, and diplomas by the R.S.P.C.A. Much credit is due to the horse-keepers for the highly satisfactory manner in which the horses appeared on parade, the aim of the committee being to encourage regular care in the feeding and grooming of horses, and to foster kindly treatment of them at all times. The committee c9nsists of Messrs. W. Bain- bridge, H. Ferguson, jun. W. Fletcher, Robert Garton, S. Picton, H. G. Roberts, W. P. Rylands, R. Spann and George Venn, with Councillor J. Many of our readers -will regret to hear of the death of Mr. John Thomas Blease, which took place on Thursday of last week at his residence,333, Lovely-lane. The deceased who was 42 years of age had been suffering since Christmas last from heart disease and dropsy. He was a faithful servant of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company, Limited, having been in the iday wages office department for upwards of 28 years. The funeral took place at the Cemetery on Monday afternoon, the officiating minister being the Rev. H. Wall, of Cockhedge Mission. Mayor's 'Warning to Publicans. • A warning to publicans was forthcoming from the Warrington Bench on Monday as the result of a csse in which Thomas Hawkins, 65, Wellington-street, was charged at the Polico Court on Monday with being drunk and 'disorderly, in Mersey-street at five minutes past eight on Saturday night, and with breaking a , glass panel at the Victoria Vaults, doing damage to the extent of 7s. 6d.-- Robert Ernest Ditchfield, bar- man, stated that at 7.45 on Saturday night, defendant , entered the house with a companion and called for a ' pint of beer for a friend. Witness served him, but defendant said he would pay the landlady. As he refused to pay witness took the beer back. Defendant then refused to leave the house, dnd Mrs. Whittaker, the landlady, put him out Defendant then broke a panel in the door with his hand, which he damaged.— Mrs. Whittaker also gave evidence, and stated that defendant was in the house at two o'clock the same afternoon, but he had been Out in the interval.- - A fine of 4s. was imposed, and the Mayor said the Bench considered defendant had been more sinned against than sinning. With regard to Mrs. Whittaker the Bench felt very strongly about defendant being allowed to enter tho house when he was so drunk that he did not know what he was doing' or ordering, and when he refused to pay was tuned out and locked up. This must be a warning to her, and he did not know what woild become of the case, which was a rather bad one. The Bench would take strict notice of cases of drunken persons leaving public-houses.— It was stated that Mr. Whittaker was ill, and could not appear.

Meeting of the Parochial Committec.

The first meeting for the year of the Stockton Heath Parochial Committee was held at the Hall Cafe on Tuesday evening when there were present :—Messrs. G. Pilling, F. Pearson, H. J. Westbrook, T. R. West- brook, J. W. Bouchier, J. Mynekyme, J. McDermid, J. Whitley, J. Hallows, G. J. Brown, F. Warburton, J. H. Clarke, W. Mclntyre, G. F'. Ashton (clerk), and J. Swinton (surveyor). On the motion• of Mr. Hallows seconded by Mr. Bouchier, Mr. Pilling was re-elected to the chair. The office of vice-chairman was again confer.ed upon Mr. Clarke.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
ALLEGED NUISANCE.

Mr. C. J. Smith, Walton-road, vt rote complaining of the nuisance arising in the neighbourhood of Walton- road and Whitefield-road, by the disposal of nightsoil on a field between the two roads. The practice was one which was both offensive and dangerous to health. He had written to Dr. Adams who had promised to move in the matter, but up to the present had not done so. He asked the Committee to take steps to prevent the continuance of the nuisance. Mr. Bouchier moved that the Committee take what- ever steps were necessary to prevent the nuisance. There was no doubt it was a great danger and he believed some sickness had already been occasioned by it: He thought the medical officer should have moved in the matter earlier. Mr. Swinton said he had seen the tenant of the field who had promised to discontinue the practice.

STET PAVING. It was decided that in future back streets and pass- ages should be paved with tarred macadam.

ROYAL INSURANCE COMPANY.

EXTENSION OF POWERS.

The Royal Insurance Company the total funds of which are over £13,500,000 and which has the distinction of being the largest fire insurance compay in the world, announces an important extension of powers. By virtue of a special Act of Parliament the Company is now in a position to undertake all classes of accident work- men's compensation, burglary, fidelity guarantee, and property owners' liability business. In view of the enormous increase in the volume of insurance which must result from the new Workmen's Compensation Act the extension of the scope of the "Royal "has been secured at a timely moment and the Company may be expected to derive material advantage.

THE NATIONAL FREE CHURCH COUNCIL AND PEACE.

The National Counoil of the Evangelical Free Churches has issued an important circular to all the local Councils of England and Wales with reference to the forthcoming Hague Peace Conference, with sugges- tions for the carrying out of a simultaneous and united effort to promote the Peace proposals that will come before the Conference. Among the recommendations are the holding of great district Council and-public gatherings for the education of public opinion and pass- ing of resolutions on the lines of that passed at the Annual Council at Leeds. The Central Office Memorial Hall will supply literature for use at these meetings. A letter is also being sent to all the Free Church Ministers asking them to observe Sunday, June 9th, as "Peace Sunday" so that special sermons may then be preached.

"t A CCOMMODATING."

A certain man, who is noted for halving an enormously thick neck, when travelling one day, found himself in need of a fresh collar, and went into a haberdasher's shop to purchase one. "I would like a collar," he said to the shop-tun. What size?" asked the latter. "Size twenty." "We don't keep collars so large as that; but I think you might be able to get one three shops further along the Etreet." He went, and found the shop indicated. It was a harness-maker's!

From your loving Nephew, REGGIE HANSON

Dolly, do go to sleep I The dark comes creepy In through the window, To make you sleepy— You don't care a bit, With your eyes open wide, About the big dark— It makes me hide! Poor, poor Arabella Can only stare; "I think, Little Mistress, It isn't fair. You know very well You bought me cheap— I wasn't made To go to sleep."

ETHEL COLLETT.

-----

AN ADVENTURE IN THE WOODS.

One day, when I was sitting under a tree in a wood I heard a rustle. On looking round, I saw that it was a snake! This startled me, and I said " Oh, dear!" and started to run. This aroused the snake, and it followed me. The snake continued the chase for about half-an- hour, but I was gaining the race, and, whilst I was running amongst the trees, the snake lost sight of me, but I saw another, and I killed this straightaway, and wandered out of the wood. WILLIAM ADAMS.

THE CAT AND THE FOX.

A fox one day said to his friend, the wild cat, "When the hounds come to hunt me, I have such tricks to get out of their way that they cannot catch me. I have ten tricks, and ten times more than you can guess." "As for poor me," said the cat, "I have only one, and, if that should fail, I am a lost cat.' ' "Alas! poor puss 1" said the fox. Just then they heard the blast of a horn, and on came the hounds in full cry. The fox ran this way anii that, and tued all his tricks, but was caught at last. The cat ran up a tree, for that was her one trick; but the hounds could not reach her. "So I see," said the cat, "that one good trick is worth mare than ten bad ones."

Dear Uncle Ned,—ln my last letter I told you I would tell you about South Australia. Well, to start with, some morning when we get up its beautiful weather, and by dinner time, dust is seen to be rising all around the hori- zon; then about 3 or 4 o'clock we have a terrible dust storm. Sometimes it gets that dark' that we have to light the lamp. I think my little cousins in England would be fright- ened if they saw it coming. In summer-time people have to be very careful, because there are so many snakes about. Sometimes in summer the heat goes up to 120 degrees in the shade, then sometimes before night it goes down to 90 degrees; this is what they call a cool change. Now I think I have told you all about South Australia this time. lam sending you a piece of poetry, called:

THE BRAVE PUPPY DOGS. Seven little_ puppy dogs sat in a row,

And boasted as will, you know; "Oh, what brave dogs are we'?" said they, "If only a rat would come this wayl

We'd toss him and tear him,

And bite him and scare him,

Till he'd wish he'd never been born, said they.

"What's that?" said one. "Tis a scratoh I hear." Then they stopped their boasting, and listened in fear. When a tiny nose peeped out from the hay, "Tis a mouse!" they all cried, and they scampered away. . .

HIS .MASTER'S HAT.

Not long ago, a fine collie dog was running happily after an omnibus, on the top of which his master was seated. Every now and then the man turned round to encourage the dog, and at last, as he did this, a gust of wind blew off his hat, which went careering down the road by the side of the omnibus. Quick as thought, the dog darted after the hat, chased it, and "rounded it up," as if it were a stray lamb or sheep, and when the man came down from the top of the omnibus to get his lost property, the dog was waiting for him, wagging his tail, with the hat safely in his mouth. Much love. DORIS Corzox.

"NEVER MIND THE BRIARS."

A man met on the road a little fellow, carry- ing a basket of blackberries, and said to him: "Sammy," where did you get such a lot of nice berries?" "Over there, sir, in the briars."

"Won't your mother be glad to see you come home, with a basket of such nice, ripe fruit?" "Yes, sir," said Sammy, with a bright smile.; "she always seems glad when I hold the berries up, and I don't tell her anything about the briars in my hand."

The man rode on. Sammy's remark had given him a lesson, and he resolved that henceforth he would try to remember the joys of life, and say nothing about its troubles.

ETHEL STEVENS.

SCIENCE NOTES AND NEWS.

LIQUID AIR FOR MINE RESCUES.

A new use for liquid air has been fund in its application to rescue work in mines. An apparatus weighing only 141 b. has been de- vised, which can be carried on the back, and which gives a supply of absolutely pure- air for about three hours. Although thus asso- ciated with collieries, it is probable that liquid air might be utilised in the same way for fire brigade service.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
PEAT:GAS FOR FURNACES.

Among the varied uses of peat, that for the production of gas is perhaps the least known. It has been used for this purpose, however, for the last thirty years in Ale steel works of Notala, in Sweden. From 13,000 to 16,000 cubic yards of peat are thus used yearly. It costs rather more than coal gas, but has the advantage of containing scarcely any sulphur or phosphorus. And in several places in Europe peat - gas is used for heating, and in glass-melting furnaces.

22,000 REVOLUTIONS A MINUTE

One of the large makers has discovered a demand for a miniature air. drill, says the Export implement Age, the size of which is no larger than a man's hand--sin. across. This midget, which weighs. 211 b., and which is modelled after the design of the larger tools, is extremely powerful, relatively, being capable of drilling up to 3-16ths of an inch steel. Its chief value is for drilling holes that have to be done accurately in pieces not otherwise readily accessible. The motor .speed is extremely high, being 22,000 revolutions per minute, while the spindle speed is more than 2,000 revolutions per minute.

PECULIARITIES OF THE INSANE EYE. The traits of the insane eye are described by Dr. E. C. Spitzka, one of the world's most famous specialists in nervous diseases. These traits, he tells us, are: (1) Permanent drooping- of one Or both upper eyelids; (2) pale, bloodless condition and unnatural colour in the whites' of the eyes; (3) pupils differing in size., one from the other, and taking irregular shapes from day to day, the form seldom following a true circle; (4) the pupils, which, in normal persons grow smaller in the presence of light, in the paretic (referring to a kind of paralytic in- sanity) eye do not respond together, one contracting more than the other, and failing in what is scientifically described as "co- ordination."

===== 0002601_19070511_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
HALLEY'S COMET,

The astronomers are bestirring themselves in preparation for the return of one of the most famous of all comets, that which bears the name of Halley. It is the greatest comet known to have been seen at more than one return, its average gtsi,l'od being about seventy-five years. It was last seen in 1835, and will, according to the most recent calcu- lations, be visible again early in 1910. The object of the astronomers at present is to predict the moment of its perihelion passage with the utmost possible accuracy. Doctor Holetschek, of Vienna, thinks it will -be visible with telescopes as early as January, 1910, and that by March everybody may see it with the naked eye. Later in the year it is likely to make a startling display, several hours before sunrise, in the northern quarter of the sky.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
" MONTEZUMA'S WELL."

Among the. many Natural curiosities of Arizona, but one that is. not often visited by ordinary tourists, is the singular bowl- shaped depression in .Y.avapai, ~County called `,` Blontezuma's Well." It is nearly circular, and between 5001 t. and .600 ft. in diameter at the brim. It lies in the midst of a nearly level area. he sides are vertical to a depth of 30ft. or 40ft. Below that they merge into a sloping talus, which extends down to a 'circular pool of water, alleged by popular tradition, as are so many other not very

deep areas of water, to be ' bottomless. This remarkable :formation has usually been de- scribed. as a "pit crater" of volcanic origin. But Professor. William P. -Blake believes it to be the result cf the falling of the roof of a cavern, formed in the limestone strata by running water. The water of the pool, he says, flows out through a subterranean channel into the valley of Beaver Creek.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DEADLY ELECTRIC WIRE.

The danger ,incurred in touching an elec- tric circuit, writes a German scientist, Herr Herman' Zipp, does not depend wholly on the, voltap or elcetfic 'tension. Two-thou- sand-volt circuits have been touched\ with ,impunity, and contact with incandescent- light circuits of only 120 volts has caused instant death.

The physiological effects of electricity are due partly to electrolysis, or decomposition of the blood and other fluids, and partly to paralysis of, vital organs. Both actions are proportional to the strength of the current that actually passes through the body, and this current strength is equal to .the voltage of the circuit touched divided by the electric resistance of the body. .The. average resist- tance measured between a 'band and the feet, when the hand is moist and tile shoes are . soaked with _ watQr, is, about 5,000 ohms. A current of 1-20th of an. ampere-1-10th of the current used in an incandescent, lamp— may cause death. Hence, the question is, Under what conditions will

CONTACT WITH AN ELECTRIC WIRE

send such a current through the body/

If the right hand touches one wire and the left hand simultaneously tenches the other wire of a 110-volt incandescent lighting cir- cuit, the body, assuming its resistance to be r94oQ„,obru,, he traversed by a caveat oS

itain,ntitv ampere, or Jane more than 1-50 t of an ampere, which is within . the limit of safety. But if the hands are dry the:resist- ance -mea-sured - from hand to hand is fully 10,000 ohms, so both wires .of even a 220-volt circuit_ such as is used in some in- candescent lighting systems, can be touched

that

WITHOUT DANGER, OF SIIRIOUS INJURY

In certain factories, however, where the air is damp, warm, and laden with acid vapours, the skin becomes softened, and the resist- ance so greatly diminished that it is not safe to touch both wires of even . a 100-volt circuit.

The writees left hand once came so neer the terminals' of a 1,000-volt transformer that it drew luminous electric arcs from them. The current, having so small a re- sistance less than 2in-. of the 'hand to

overcome, was very strong, but it caused no injury except local bums, because it did not traverse a vital part. In instructive con- trast to this ease is the experience of the in- ventor of an electric bath tub containing metal plates that ,could be connected with a 220-volt lighting circuit. The inventor tested the dilice on himself, and

WAS INSTANTLY KILLED, because soaking in water had so reduced the resistance of his body that even this low .voltage produced a deadly current. If a man; standing on a trolley rail or even on the ground, touches a broken trolley \vire. carrying .the usual pressure of about 500 volts, a current of 1-10th of an ampere will ,pass through his body, if its resistance is 5,000 -ohms, and will probably cause death. In this case the ground or the rail represents the second or return wire. It is much

LESS DANGEROUS TO TOUCH A SINGLE WIRE of a double-wire circuit, well insulated from the ground, for in order to reach the other wire the current that traverses the body must flow through or over some of the in- sulators, that is to say, through a very great resistance, so that,. the current is very weak. But in a very long line the :um of these little leakage currents through Many in- sulators may be too great tor a Littman °Gay to carry with safety. , Hence, the danger is proportional to the length of the line. Simultaneous Contact with both wires of a high-voltage circuit is, of course, fatal.

THEFT FROM A REGISTRY OFFICE.

On Tuesday at Prescot, Jane Westhead, married,'of Widnes, was charged with stealing a purse containing five postal orders' of the total value of £3 N., the property of Miss Constance Fogg, registrar, Preecot. On the 4th inst. prisoner went-to the office to register the death of a relative, and dtiring the absence of Miss Fogg from the room took the purse from a stationery cabinet, left the office, and drove back to Widnes. Prisoner was bound over under the First Offenders Act in her own recognisance%

ORFORD BARRACKS V. WILDERSPOOL BREWERY.— Played at the Barracks on Saturday, the home team being victorious by 26 runs. Score :—Orford Barracks 97; Wilderspool Brewery, 69. BEWSEY WESLEYANS V. CHESHIRE LINES ENGINEERS. =This match was played at Arpley on Saturday. The end was exciting as the visitors required five runs to win with four wickets to fall and, failing to get them, lost by three runs. J. Duckworth made 16 for the visit- ing team and R. WARRINGTON DISTRESS COMMITTEE. ---- Government Asked to Confer Greater Powers.

The Warrington Distress Committee met on Monday evening under the presidency of Alderman Tinnion. There were also present :—Lev. W. Bracecamp, Miss Broadbent, Councillors Stott, Dickenson and Barton, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, T. P. Bradsbaw, C. Haley S. Davies, A. T. Hallaway (deputy town clerk), and J. Fargher (inquiry officer.) ELECTIONS.

Mr. Bradshaw was elected a member of the Finance and General Purposes Committee in place of Councillor Steel who ceased to be a member of the Distress Com- mittee on his election to the Council. All the co-optative members were re-elected to their respective sub-committees.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
ESTIMATE OF EXPENDITURE.

At a meeting of the Finance and General Purposes Committee the report of Mr. Ernest W. Hicks' district auditor, was read. It stated that the total receipts during the year ended 31st March, 1606, amounted to £54 18s. 2d., being raised by precept upon the borough. The expenditure confined to salaries and establishment charges amounted to £55 3s. 2d. The estimate of expenditure for the ensuing year, amounting to £2OO, was aproved. The minutes were passed. INCREASED POWERS WANTED. THE QUESTION OF EMIGRATION, Mr. J. D. BUCKTON waited upon the Inquiry and Labour Committee with reference to an application made on behalf of the Soldiers' • and Sailors' Help Society as to the use of the exchange by, soldiers and sailors who have left the service. After hearing his explanation, it was resolved that the application be complied with for a period of twelve menths, and that the Mayor be requested to allow his name to be placed in the Society's handbook as the local " friend." The Mayor notified his willingness to accede to this request. The 'Committee considered the question of aiding emigra- tion, and it was resolved that the Inquiry Officer be instructed to make all necessary inquiries into the cases of applicants desirous of emigrating, and report to a further meeting of this Committee. It was resolved that this Committee desires to call attention to the following facts :—(1) That the number as registered under the Unemployed Workmen's Act, 1905, does not represent the total number of unemployed, the numerous unnecessary and irritating questions attaching to an application for relief prevent many of the most deserving registering ; (2) That Committees are greatly hampered both in providing sufficient per- missible employment for applicants and in obtaining the necessary funds therefor, and urges upon the Government the necessity of immediately introducing a Bill to amend the said Act; that this Committee is further of opinion that the cost of providing employ- ment under the Act should not be dependent upon volun- tary contributions. That a copy of this resolution be sent to the Prime Minister, the President of the Local Government Board, and the Member for the Borough. Mr. HALEY asked if any reply had been received from the Borough member. The DEPUTY TOWN CLERK said a formal acknow- ledgment had been received. The minutes were approved. THE INQUIRY OFFICER..

Mr. HALEY said he believed the period for which the services of Mr. Fargher were continued had almost expired. Would it be in order for the committee to consider the matter now?

The CHAIRMAN said the appointment of Mr. Fargher was for a further term of six months and that would expire in June. There was no doubt that that most importantmatter would be taken in hand by the Inquiry and Labour Committee and the appointment would probably be made for a further term. He thought they were all agreed that the services rendered by Mr. Fargher had been excellent and he had done his duty with great tact and judgment. He did not suppose the committee would hesitate to re-appoint such a capable officer. As the appointment was made by the sub-com- mittee and confirmed by the Distress Committee and as no notice had been given, they could not deal with e mstter now.

WARRINGTON

Coming Events. As announced in our advertisement columns, anniversary services will be held at St* John's, Winwick-street, to-morrow (Sunday), when the preacher will ' be the Rev. A. de Vlieger, Litt. D., of Chorlton-cum-Hardy. At the present time, when cordial relations are being established between the British and Dutch peoples in the Transvaal, it may be of interest to state that Dr. de Vlieger is by birth a native of Holland, and was first engaged in ministerial work in connection with a Dutch foreign mission. He las, however, lived and laboured in many lands. He xecived his degree of Literarum Doctor from the University of Lausanne, Switzerland, and was ordained a Presbyterian minister at Cairo. Since 1904 he has been pastor of the Presbyterian Church at Chorlton- cum-Hardy, and a popular and respected member of the Manchester Presbytery. The annual sale of work in connection with St. Barnabas' Ohurch will be held in the Conservative Club Assembly Room, Sankey-street, on Wednesday' and Thursday next and will be opened on the respee- tive days by Mrs. J. D. Buckton and Miss White. The proceeds will be in aid of the Church Extension Fund. ,A meeting in support of the Women's Suffrage movement will be held in Toft's 'Assembly Rooms on 'Tuesday afternoon. Addresses will be delivered by Mrs. Alan Bright ond Miss Lucy Broadbent, M.A. Manchester. The Infirmary and Dispensary. The Hon. Treasurer begs to acknowledge, with thanks, the gift by Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., of the sum of £l4 6s. 6d., being proceeds of sale of tickets for fishing in the Walton Reservoir.
