Hollinaworth ex-police sergeant. , Dr. Barnado's Homes.

An unusually attractive entertainment was provided in the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, last evening, by a contingent of boys from Dr. Barnado's homes. these participants in the beneficent operations of this philanthopic institution proved themselves very capable musicians and entertainers and their efforts were warmly applauded. The Mayor presided over a large attendance and during an interval an address on the works of Dr. Barnado's Homes was delivered by Mr. F. E. Rainer, lime-light illustrations adding consider- ably to the lecture. A special word of praise is due to Mrs. C. Jackson Holmes upon Whom the local arrangements have largely devolved. Teachers' Visit to Warrington Works. On Saturday the members of the Warrington • Certificated Class Teachers' Association paid a visit to Whitecross Works. On arrival the party, which numbered about 60, was met by the heads of the various departments by whom they were conducted round the establishment, interesting and lucid explana- tions being given of the different operations in the manufacture of wire, wire netting, nails, ez.e. The visit was not prompted by idle curiosity but was one of a series to the manufactories of the town inaugurated with a view to obtaining practical information which cannot be found in text books, and which is of the greatest value in giving lessons on local industries to scholars attending the Warrington elementary schools. The object is commendable and on every hand the employers have welcomed the teachers' visits. Women's Liberal Federation. The annual Council meetings of the Women's Liberal Federation are to be held on Tuesday, Wednesday, and Thursday next week in the Memorial Hall, London. Among other basiness resolutions will he moved in favour of Women's Suffrage, the appointment of women on local governing bodies, temperance (licensing reform, local veto, and Sunday closing), the reformation of the House of Lords, Old- age Pensions, and a host of other democratic and progressive measures. The representatives of the Warrington Women's Liberal Association who will attend the Federation meetings are Mrs. Osborne (hon. secretary), Mrs. P. Maginn, Mrs. Lindley, Mrs. I Simpson, Mrs. C. E. Parker, and Mrs. P. C. Garnett. PUBLIC BENEFIT, UNDER THE CLOCK FOR BEST Roars IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt. -Federation of Free Church Councils. The Chester, Crewe, and District Federation of Free Church Councils paid a visit to Runcorn on Thursday. There was a good attendance of delegates. In the morning, at Bethesda Church, Dr. J. Rendell Harris. president of the National Council, delivered a Biblical lecture. Afterwards the Right Hon. Sir J. T. Brunner, Bart, M.P., entertained the delegates at ; luncheon. In the afternoon a conference was held in ' .St. Parrs Morning Chapel, and the Rev. T. B. Field, of Crewe, the Federation secretary, read a paper on "How to Help our Federation to be of Greater 'Service to the Councils." A sermon was then delivered by the Rev. J. Dodd Jackson, of Crewe. In the evening a public meeting was held in St. Paul's I Wesleyan Church. Mr. W. Handley, President of the local Council, occupied the chair, and the principal speakers were Dr. Harris and Dr. Hodgson, C.C„ of Crewe.

X iss Lancashire Limited. " Miss Lancashire Limited " still continues to find great favour with the theatre-going public, and there is little doubt that the splendid success which attended its last production in Warrington will be reneated next week when the play will be presented for six nights in the Parr Hall. Miss Florence Baines, the talented lady who, it may rightly be said, created the part of the Lancashire Lass again heads the list of artistes and Warrington people will not miss the opportunity of again seeing her, especially as in the immediate future she will embark upon a lengthy visit to Australia and America. Hague Conference At the meeting of the Workingmen's Mission Christian Endeavour Society held last week, the following resolutions were unanimously adopted "That this meeting heartily congratulates his Majesty's Government upon its determination to bring the question of arresting the increase of armaments before the Hague Conference, and earnestly desires that the British representatives at the Conference may obtain (1) Universal obligatory arbitration for all secondary questions which do not involve National interests • (2) A Peace Budget by which one pound will be devoted to peace propaganda and International hospitality for every £l,OOO spent on the Army and Navy ; (2) The boycotting of any Power that resorts to hostilities without first trying to avert war by calling in special mediators as recom- mended by Article 8 of the Hague Convention." It was also resolved "That copies of the foregoing resolutions be signed by the Chairman on behalf of this meeting, and forwarded to Sir Henry Campbell- Bannerman, Sir Edward Grey, the Marquis of Lans- downe, Mr. Arthur J. Balfour- and the Borough Member (Mr. A. H. Crosaeld,

The second excursion of the Warrington Branch of the E.L.T.L.V. took place on Saturday last to Flaltm Castle. A fair number of members attended. After the performance of a few chapters of the "Dream of the future " Mr. Bagot of St. Annes-on-the-Sea, one of the oldest and staunchest single taxers, addressed the audience in a fiery speech pointing out that until we had the single tax, every invention, every improvement, all the rates and taxes on labour and industries would find their way into the pockets of the land- lord, and we ought to do therefore all in our power to abolish all rates and taxes and replace them by a single tax on land values, the same that we are paying to-day in land-rent to the landlord. That would ' increase trade and industry, banish unemployment,. raise wages to their true limit, bring all land to proper use and spread prosperity and happinness over the world.He explained that it is not to Parliament we

have to look for help ; the battle has to,be fought in every town, here in Warrington. His _speech was

given -with such a warmth and such a force as we have never heard before, and we can only regret that Mr. Bagot is not oftener coming to preach the new

... . crusade: against an old mighty evil

A house to

house distbution of literatui:e airoughout the village will have helped in spreading the light.lt was resolved by the members to have the next excursion on Saturday June Ist to Frodsham Hill leaving Bank Quay by the the 3.19 p.m. train. CIiLLITLAR SHIRTS from 2/6—BANKs,Outfitter.-Advt.

Vandeville Entertainment at Parr Hall. An excellent Vandeville entertainment is again this week provided by Mr. H. E. Barnes in the Parr Hall, and throughout the performances have been witnessed

by large— audiences. All round the programme submitted is a good one and contains no weak spots. The outstanding item is perhaps the musical melange by the Albano Troupe and selections from Zampa rendered on a concertina by a member of the quartette are particularly well given. " The tree of truth" a farcical sketch by Harry Braham and Co., provides no little amusement, whilst Miss Rose Elliott is a distinct success in songs which appeal to the public taste. Wal Robbins as a female impersonator provides a unique turn and displays unusual ability. Hat balancing feats of a difficult character are performed by Friscaii who is in the front rank of artistes who are exponents of this parti- cular art, and the Katos provide an attractive turn. Bernard and Weston with their patter and songs create considerable laughter, whilst Mooney and Holbein, the grotesque dancer and singer,in a Vaudeville inter- mezzo are much to the fore. The adventures of a runaway motor car and of a traveller who spen3s the night at a haunted hotel are depicted by the cine- matograph which has become an indespensable item in variety entertainments. The musical accompani- ments and instrumental selections, are provided by a capable orchestra under the direction of Mr.W.A.Guest, R.A.M. The first performance on L hursday was witnessed by the members of the victorious War- rington Football team. The performances will be repeated this evening at 7 and 9 o'clock. Institute of Journalists. A special general meeting of the members of the Liverpool District of the Institute of Journalists was held at the Warrington Town Hall on Saturday afternoon to consider the reform scheme, affecting the organization of the Institute. Mr. F. Fennelly (Runcorn) presided over a representative attendance. After considerable discussion the meeting adopted a number of recommendations, the most important being That an Executive Council should take the place of Council and executive, the new body not to exceed 1 twenty in number, and to be elected by direct general postal ballot by districts or groups of districts. That the districts have complete financial control in their own areas, with power to invite members able to do so to contribute larger amounts than the current sub- , scription, and the authority to remit or pay subscrip- tions for members in temporarily embarassed cir- cumstances, and to remove members from the roll, subject to appeal to the Executive Council. That the • following resolution now before the Special Committee be approved : —" That while the Conference gladly; welcomes any scheme for the institute,it would recom- mend to the Council the consideration of the desira- bility of the taking of actuarial advice, and send- jug to each district for its consideration a sliding scale tables, showing the subscriptions whieh would be necessary to provide each member with sick and out- , of-work benefits, as well as securing to him the present advantages of the institute; such table to be based on any increased cost that will be incurred for office and organizing expenses." That, subject to the approval of the Council, districts be empowered to come to arrangements with Universites for the con_ erring of degrees in Journalism or of diplomas for fuch knowledge as may be useful to the profession. As vote of thanks was passed to the Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) for the use of the meeting room.

"THE WHITE CITY" IN THE MAKING.

The transformation which the old Botanical Gardena at Old Trafford is undergoing strikes the -visitor privi- leged to witness it as in the nature of an incident from "The Arabian Nights." "The White City" which, in the course of the next ten days, will have com- pletely replaced the various Herbarium on the site, will be a dream of attractiveness,and will give to Manchester a centre of interest in a suburb which has long awaited the corning of something of the kind. At the present moment some 900 men are employed night and day in the erection of the various buildings, the installation of the electric plant, the Hiram-Maxium flying-machine, the tremendous water-chute with its lake and the numerous rivulets which intersect the entire grounds for the passage of gondolas and other pleasure boats. Of the extent of the work being done— work which commenced only so recently as the 25th February last—some idea may be gained when it is stated that up to Tuesday last there have been used some 15 tons of white enamel paint, 270 tons of white plaster and about 300,000 cubic feet of timber, while the electric installation accounts for 40,000 lamps of varying candlepower, and about 20 tons of copper in one electric main around the gardens. The enterprise is, we understand, essentially an English one, the whole scheme being carried out under the managing director- ship of Mr. J. C. Brown. As wilt be seen from an advertisement on our first page, ten hours of amusements are offered in a round of attractions to be thrown open to the public on Whit- Monday. A long list of novelties is announced, and though the typical Britisher proverbially works hard at his play it will be difficult for him to exhaust the resources of the" White City."lt will not be surnrising if Whit-Monday at "White City'' ma de a record in the matter of numbers who pass the turnstiles at a single place of amusement.

He appealed to them, as democrats, to give to Irishmen the freedom they had given the Boers. (Applause.) —Mr. Max IVluspratt supported, and referred to the criticism of the Member for the division on the Govern- ment's proposals for Army reform. He said Mr. Haldane had taken the Army in hand on sound commercial and scientific lines. He had set himself the problem of find- ing and ramoving its weak points, and strengthening what remained. Beyond that of Mr. Arnold-Forster, who had a counter scheme which did not come off, there had been no real criticism of the scheme. If Colonel Walker saw the ruin of the Army pending, he should be in his place to make his criticism of Mr. Haldane across the floor of the House of Commons, and not do Jo in the back room of the local Conservative club. (Hear, henr.)—The resolution was carried unanimously.

Tne'bill made an attempt to deal with the difficulty Of excessive size by a process of selection, caused by a modification of the hereditary principle. The sugges- tion was that writs were to be issued only to peers who under the bill were termed qualified. The qualification entitling an hereditary peer to a writ of summons was the holding at any time of one of the high offices in the State specified in the schedule. The unqualified peers would elect one-fourth of their number at the beginn- ing of each Parliament, on the same principle as that which prevailed in regard to Scottish peers. That principle of delegation appeared to him to meet the difficulty of selection, and also of unfit and absentee peers. The house consisted, roughly, of 600 peers, of whom eighty-two ; had never taken the oath. Last session the largest number in attendance on any one day was 312, and the Private Bill Committee work was performed by less I than 100 peers. There was a large balance of what he I might term redundant peers, who might disappear without any great harm being done— (laughter)—and who never would be missed as regarded the division lists. (Laughter.) The position of the reluctant peer who was averse to political life, was ' one of the most remarkable in the world, because while monarchs might abdicate and directors of public com- panies could be forced to retire if they did not attend , to their duties, the rule with peers was once a peer ' always a peer. No doubt an immense amount of rubbish ; was talked on Radical platforms about the privileged , classes, but nobody could dispute the fact of the exist- • ence of the privilege of being born with aright to legis- late ; and if persons born with this right did not choose ' to exercise it, he did not see that they had cause of , complaint if the privilege was taken from them. I Answering the objection that the principle of delegation ; would make the House more Tory than ever, he said he I felt it was impossible to turn the house into a Liberal ' assembly. He believed that if 400 or 500 tradesmen or stockbrokers were put into the phces of the present peers, they would in the long run arrive at very much the same decisions as the existing cuamber. If 400 or 500 gentlemen from the Government side of the present House of Commons were transplanted to the House of Lords, they would witntss an extraordinary change in their opinions. (Laughter.) The task of keeping Liberalism alive in that chamber was difficult enough. (Laughter.) Even sturdy democrats introduced there showed signs of wear and tear and deterioration very soon. (Laughter.) Still, the bill showed the utmost solicitude for the Liberal party, because he calculated that under the bill the number of Liberal peers would be increased from seventy at present to at least 150. He estimated roughly that the num- ber of qualified peers would be 230, and the repre- sentative peers would be between 90 and 100. On the principle of equality he invited the spiritual peers to make some some sacrifice in members and he only hoped that the right rev. Prelate, who always appeared to represent the permanent Opposition, would find a seat in a reconstituted assembly. (Laughter). The late Lord Salisbury suggested there should be categorical peers, but the-real difficulty and objection to that proposal was that if once a oateaory of various classes was laid down, they would be laced with the difficulty of the groat unrepresented. They heard, for instance, that Nonconformists should be represented in the house, but there were something like 250 denominations in this country, and if they selected from one denomination why should net the Salvation Army claim representation ? If they opened it to repre- sentatives of the arts and sciences and the drama, why not to those vocations represented by the " mudded oaf and the flannelled fool ?" It would be far more practical to leave the decision of who were to be life peers to the Government of the day. The responsibility should rest with the Government, and be would never have any' fear that they would appoint ' improper persons. As to Clause 7, which enabled peers who were not returned as representative to stand for the House of Commons,overyone had seen the injustice under which unrepresentative Scotch peers were placed. , They were a sort of political derelicts, classed with paupers, lunatics, and criminals a position which was • eminently unfair and unjust. He was hardly prepared ,to deal with the amendment put down in the name of the Government in a serious spirit,because it amounted to a barefaced refusal to deal with the question, although they had asserted for many years that it ought •to be dealt with as soon as possible. He had carefully abstained from touching upon the differences which unfortunately prevailed between the two houses,becanse he regarded them as a separate question, and there was . no reason whatever why it should be treated in a party spirit. (Cheers.)

ST. HELENS.

SHOCKING GLASSWORKS ACCIDENT.

William Brown, aged fifty-five, of York-street, St. Helens, met with a terrible accident on Monday at Messrs. Pilkington's sheet-glass works. Brown was working among the machinery when his right arm was caught, and he was whirled off his feet. Hir arm was so badly shattered that Dr. Siddall and his colle4gue found it necessary to amputate the limb before the unfortu- nate man could be removed to hospital.

PRIMITIVE METHODIST SYNOD.

1 The delegates and friends attending the Liverpool District Synod of the Primitive Methodist Connexion at St. Helens had a very busy week-end. On Saturday an open-air meeting near the Market-place was followed , by a public meeting in the Westfield-street Chapel, Mr. W. Charnock, of Accrington, presiding. The Rev. W. Spedding, of Birkenhead, delivered an interesting lecture on" The Christian Endeavour at its Best." This was the age, he said, of dying enthusiosm. The opinion ,of men were always changing. Old enthusiasms were • always dying and new ones were taking their place. Of late years there had sprung up splendid new institutions There was the P,S.A. movement adult Bible classes,ancl the Christion Brotherhood Union, and also the great Christian Endeavour movement. The Christian , Endeavour is a great world-wide movement which seeks to accomplish three things—Spiritual culture in young people. and Christian brotherhood among young ; people. The Christian Endeavour was esssea- ; tially a young people's spiritual movement.— God had given to every man a greit commission. He had given into every man's keeping his manhood, and to every, woman her womanhood, which was an even greater 'commission than the keeping of the firmaments. Christian Endeavour did not create men of self-centred piety and weakly sentimentalists, but men of strength and virility. It created a spirit of social regeneration and philanthrophy in its young people, and made them • true citizens who will some day take their places in the • religious and public life of the nation.—The Rev. J. Watkin.of Liverpool, gave a witty and eloquent address on the "Temperance Question Up-to-date." He said the temperance reformers of to-day had to fight a great and ancient prejudice—that it wa impossible for a man to be healthy, wealthy, and wise without recourse to alcoholic stimulants. This prejudice ' was entrenched in all departments of life, and even in the Christian Church itself. The race of life was to the strong and virile, and England could not hope to hold her own among the peoples of the world if her people were anemic and weak. A few doctors had just I issued a manifesto in favour of taking alcohol as a tonic ! or medicine, but in opposition to those few there were 15,000 doctors who say that the use of alcohol inter- fered, with the power of the intellect and the use of the hands. It was idle to say that the use of alcohol was bar the good of the country's trade. No country could call itself wealthy when the majority of its citizens ' were on the verge of starvation. The Rev. J. Gillender, •of Chorley, also gave a telling address on the Sunday school of to-day. On Sunday no less than ten meetings and services were held. At 9.30 an open-air service was held in Victoria-square, addressed by the Rev F. W. Brett, of Preston. In the afternoon the centenary camp meeting was held at Denton's Green. The Rev. J. Gillender and Mr. J. Kendall were the conductors, nd there were a large number of speakers.

PAST SEASON REVIEWED.

Once again the annual review of the work of the I Warrington Rugby Football Club is to be opened with the expression of pardonable pride in their striking success in the Northern Union Cup Competition. The triumph is still fresh in the minds of Warrington enthusiasts, and so much has been written and said about it, that it is unnecessary here to add more than that the splendid victory over Oldham has proved the team to be the strongest in the Union, and one which can worthily uphold the past and best traditions of the club. Floreat Wirepallers ! The opening of the season 1900-7 was regarded with no little trepidation in many quarters, and there were many who were pessimistically inclined to the view that the future of the handling code was hanging in the balance. Years of unpopular rules coupled with the awkward efforts of the responsible Committee to effect a reformation had resulted in the game, from the point of view of the spectators, degenerating into an uninteresting exhibition con- sisting of a monotonous succession of scrummages and a depressing style of play in which tall kicking and sheer strength took precedence of more open and attractive tactics. Money makes the mare to go, and the constant cry raised by the Clubs of decreasing revenue awakened the Northern Union Committee to a sense of their responsibilities and they,l so to speak,adopted a kill or cure attitude. New and drastic regulations were introduced and at the elose of the season it can safely be said that the cure has been effected and with few exceptions a gratifying re-awakening of interest is to be reported in Northern Union Rugby circles. The re-habilitation of the game in popular favour is not yet, however, complete and further modification of the laws is necessary and will doubtless be forthcoming. This should be directed principally against the sytem of placing the clubs in the League table. As was urged last year, followers ;of the game prefer the old system of points to the mathematical puzzle presented by the present reckon- ing of percentages. Moreover the old practice prel eluded the possibility of teams playing 24 matches and I winning 17, taking precedence over clubs which defeat 23 out of 34 opponents as in the case of Keighley and ;Wigan this season. A suggestion has been made for the remodelling of the League, and has met with general favour. It is that Clubs shall meet all teams in the ; County in which they are situated and in addition shall play at least four matches with clubs from other ' shires. At the close of the season the four clubs ' which occupy the leading positions, will,as during this Iseason, meet to decide which shall be the champions. , The idea has much to commend it,not the least being the prevention of clubs selecting a list of light fixtures and occupying a position to which they are not entitled by merit. Warrington opened their season in a remarkable manner and in their first six home matches established the unique record of scoring thirty points or over against each of their opponents. This,however,was too good to last and injuries to players reducing the strength of the team,they fell from their high position , and slowly but surely the prospect of League honours ; became dimmed and finally disappeared altogether. At hardly any period between the opening and close of the season was the full three-quarter line on the effective list and as one player recovered another was placed hors de combat with the , result that the combination suffered and prospective I victories were turned into defeats. Cup-tie experi- ences commenced early and December Ist saw the team at Wigan opposing Broughton Rangers in the ILancashire Cup final. In this match their high and ireasonable hopes of success were dashed to the ground in the first few minutes as Captain Fish met with serious injury and playing a man short they had perforce to bow the knee to their opponents. Fish sustained a broken arm and his absence for some ten Iweeks proved a great handicap to the team. Incident- ally it emphasised the need of Warrington's securing a really speedy threequarter as a reserve man. An lemergency man who can come within a second of even time would -prove a valuable asset to the club and, iin fact, is indispensable. The forwards also after I Christmas began to show some signs of the hard work I they had undergone and the Committee would be -wise to secure two or three men fit for first-class corn- ipany in order to strengthen a reserve front rank which is at present inexperienced. Preston's falling off was, in particular, most marked and the burly Swintonian ,in the concluding stages had to be dropped I altogether. In the opening rounds of the Northern I Union Cup Competition Warrington, with a somewhat I weakened team, were fortunate in being drawn at , home, the advantage so gained compensating for the I absence of prominent backs. Batley were the first to I receive the quietus, and Warrington avenged past defeats by running up a score of 34 points to 9. Hull and Huddersfield also succumbed and on April 13th Swinton were vanquished in the semi-final by 21 points to nil. With all the players fit—with the exception of Jenkins—the strenuous preparations for the final were rewarded by the lifting of the Cup. The Warrington team has fulfilled 31 League engagements, won 21, lost 12, and drawn one, scoring 551 points against 304. The teams who defeated Warrington were Broughton Rangers, Salford,Flunslet, Wigan, Leeds, Swinton, Runcorn, Huddersfield, Oldham and Halifax, the last two clubs being the only ones to win both home and away matches. Warring- ton's highest score was 52 points to nil run up against Liverpool, and their heaviest defeat was sustained at Halifax where they retired vanquished by a margin of 35 points. Hull have the best reason to remember Warrington's prowess as they have been defeated three times during the season, one occasion being in the Cup Competition. Widnes, St. Helens, Leigh, Huddersfield, Rochdale, Swinton, Liverpool and Bradford have been defeated twice either in League fixtures or cup-ties whilst Broughton Rangers, Salford, Barrow, Wigan, Oldham, Hunslet, Leeds and Runcorn have each been made to acknowledge Warrington's superiority on one occasion. Turning to the players the season opened with Berry at full back and early on the Warrington lad showed form which placed him in the first rank of custodians. With an eye to eventual- ities the committee also secured the services of Tilley, from Widnes, as a reserve back,and'for a time he was played in the threequarter line. Here he was not a success and the members were questioning the wisdom of the committee when his opportunity came. Berry was injured and was replaced by Tilley and from the first the ex-Widnesian justified his selection and proved his sterling worth. Fish particularly in the latter part of the season, has hardly played up to his reputation. In fact many of his admirers have mournfully muttered "Thy glory has departed from thee." Lack of training was largely to blame and with the exception of a few spasmodic flashes he showed none of his former brilliancy. At the very close of the season he, however, made amends and his sensational try in the Cup-final atoned for previous shortcomings and re-iistated him as the idol of the team. It is some twelve years since Isherwood first joined Warrington's ranks but the passing of time leaves no mark upon him and to-day he is play- ing with as much dash as of old and has lost none of his cleverness and resource. As with the rest of the backs the season has not dealt kindly with him and injuries of a more or less serious nature have from time to time kept him from the field of play, but in spite of his injuries he has played remarkably well and is still one of the mainstays of the team. War- rington is well served by Widnes lads and Taylor at right centre has contributed in no small measure to the success of the team. He has hardly the individual brilliance of Dickenson but of the two has shown the isetter combination. He and his wing have had that understanding which leads to tries. Brooks' play has been characterised with the old determination and solid work, and as a try getter he has the honour of heading the list. For the greater part of the season the team has been served at half by Lees and Jenkins and as a pair it would be difficult to improve upon them. Jenkins, who comes from Wales, in particular, has covered himself with glory and is regarded as the finest "catch " ever made by the Committee since Fish's advent. Hocken- hall has been as erratic as ever. Could he maintain consistent form he would be one of the best halves in the Union, but unfortunately he does not always come off. Great praise is however due to him for his display in the cup ties. A pleasing feature of the season has been the form of the forwards, who throughout have done well; and adapted themselves to the altered conditions of the game. Considering the amount of work they are now called upon to do, " staleness " has not been unduly marked, and with one or two.good reserves there is no need to be apprehensive of the future.

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

EATLESTOWN AND NEWTON

Child Drowned In a Moat.

Mr. F. A. Jones, deputy coroner, held an inquest at the Chapel House Inn. Great Sankey, on Tuesday, touching the death,of William Richard Roberts, aged one year and five months, son of John Arthur Roberts, iron bedstead maker, Old Barrow Hall, Bold. The evidence showed that on Sunday Mr. Roberts and three of his sons were gardening when they missed deceased who had been playing about. On search being made he was found in a moat, one foot deep, which surrounds the house, and the bank of which is very steep. Artificial respiration was applied but unsuccessfully and when Dr. Murray arrived he pro- nounced life extinct.—A verdict of " Accidental death "was returned.

===== 0002601_19070511_art0120.txt | included=true | included =====
THENEW CHIEF CONSTABLE. WATCH COMMITTEE'S APPOINTMENT.

The Watch Committee of the Warrington Corpora- tion held a special meeting yesterday afternoon for the appointment of Chief Constable of the Borough in succession to the late Mr. Luke Talbot. The Mayor (Alderman Smethurst) presided over a full attendance. The eight, candidates selected to appear before the' committee were :—Superintendent Bestwick, New- castle-on-Tyne; Mr. J. W. A.. Danby, Chief Constable of Hyde; Inspector Dawson, Liverpool; Mr. W. E. Jones, chief officer of the headquarters staff, Brad- ford ; Superintendent P. Monaghan, Warrington: Mr. Martin Nicholls, Chief Constable of Windsor; Mr. J. H. Watson, Chief Constable of Congleton ; Inspector Weatherhogg, Rotherham. After the candidates had been interviewed and their merits carefullY considered, the choice of the committee fell upon Mr. Martin Nicholls, Chief Constable of Windsor, who in the end was unanimously appointed. The commencing salary is £350 per annum. The appointment will be submitted for confirmation at the next meeting of the Town Council and in the meantime it will no doubt came before the General Purposes Committee. It is, expected that Mr. Nicholls will enter upon his duties in a month or five weeks.

Mr. Nicholls is a married man, thirty-six years of age, and is very well built, standing fift. Pinches high. He has had sixteen years thoroughly practical police experience and has held the office of Chief Con- stable al Windsor for upwards of five years. He joined the Reading County Borough Police Force as a constable on February 10th, 1891, and rapidly- passed through the various grades of the service until in September, 1899 he was promoted to inspector and chief clerk. He held the position for two years when on September Ist. 1901, he was appointed Chief Constable of Windsor from a large number of applicants. He has also fulfilled the duties of Inspector of Hackney Carriages, Inspector under the Explosives Acts, and Billet Master at Windsor. Mr. Nicholls possesses the St. John Ambul- ance Association certificate and the Life Saving Society's medal for life saving and resuscitation,and he has also had the honour of being presented with the certificate on vellum of the Royal Humane Society for a rescue from drowning. During his service in Windsor many important functions have taken place and on several occasions large detachments of the Metropolitan Police have been employed under his direction. Mr. Nicholls has been highly complimented upon the police arrangements made when immense crowds have had to be dealt with. There are ten race meetings at Windsor annually and throughout thfr year there are many visitors to the tow*, over 24,000 having viewed the State apart- ments of Windsor Castle during one day, and when his Majesty is in residence notable personages are daily arriving or departing from the Castle. It will thus be seen that Mr. Nicholls has had a wide experience of police work in the Royal borough, and Press notices which he submitted to the Watch Committee bear testimony to the excellence of his arrangements on such memorable occasions as the visit of the King and Queen of Italy, the marriage of Prin- cess Alice of Albany and Prince Alexander of Teck, the visits of the King and Queen of Portugal and the King of Spain, the marriage of Princess Margaret and Prince Gustavus of Sweden, the visits of the King of the Hellenes and the King and Queen of Norway. Mr. Nicholls is also warmly praised for the manner in which he has coped with the traffic at the Royal Ascot races.

Mr. James Brown, Mayor of Windsor, as Chair- man of the Watch Committee, in a testimonial said: Mr. Nicholls is a most efficient and zealous officer, and has given every satisfac- tion here. His removal would certainly be a distinct loss to Windsor, but for his sake we shall be glad to hear of his promotion to the post he now seeks and for which we consider him eminently well qualified in every respect. The whole of the resident justices of the peace for the Borough of New Windsor in a very eulogistic letter described Mr. Nicholls as a highly capable and efficient officer.

• Sir William Shipley. Kt., J.P., Mayor of Windsor, 1902-3, 1903-4, 1904-s, and County Councillor for Berkshire, wrote: We shall be very sorry to lose him for I do not think it would be possible to get a better Chief Constable for this borough. His duties are carried out with great tact and discretion.

LOSS OF THE JEBHII.

The Captain Censured.

The Board of Trade inquiry into the loss of the Elder Dempster steamer Jebba, which went ashore on March 18th on the Devon coast, near Salcombe, was con- cluded on Thursday, when judgment was given. The Court found that the disaster was due to the vessel having overrun her distance, caused by the. failure of Captain Mills, the master, to make proper use of his lead. Had soundings been taken more frequently he would have been warned of his approach to land in time to avoid the calamity. The Court found that the casuality was due to the wrongful act and default of the master alone. His certificate was suspended for three months.
